\mathsf{Qd}^{(H,H^{\prime})}=\big(\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muM\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(H,H^{\prime})},\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(H,H^{\prime})}\big)=\mathsf{Qd}_{f^{(H,H^{\prime})},g^{(H,H^{\prime})}}\,.

We let
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbf{p}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(H,H^{\prime})}:I^{(H,H^{\prime})}\to\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muM\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(H,H^{\prime})}
be the
canonical projection,
\mu^{(H,H^{\prime})}
be the area measure of
\mathsf{Qd}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
, and, whenever they exist,

\gamma^{(H,H^{\prime})}
,
\bar{\gamma}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
be the maximal geodesics,
\xi^{(H,H^{\prime})}
,
\bar{\xi}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
be the shuttles.

We refer to Figure
15
for an illustration of the following proposition in the upcoming context of random quadrilaterals in the Brownian plane.

Proposition 39
.

Let
0\leq H<H^{\prime}<H^{\prime\prime}\leq-\inf_{I}f
be in the extended positive real
line. Then

\mathsf{Qd}^{(H,H^{\prime\prime})}=G\big(G\big(\mathsf{Qd}^{(H,H^{\prime})},\mathsf{Qd}^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})};\xi^{(H,H^{\prime})},\gamma^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}\big);\bar{\gamma}^{(H,H^{\prime})},\bar{\xi}^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}\big)\,,

(46)

and it holds that

\displaystyle\gamma^{(H,H^{\prime\prime})}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{(H,H^{\prime})}\cup\big(\gamma^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}\setminus\xi^{(H,H^{\prime})}\big)\,,

\displaystyle\xi^{(H,H^{\prime\prime})}

\displaystyle=\xi^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}\cup\big(\xi^{(H,H^{\prime})}\setminus\gamma^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}\big)\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\gamma}^{(H,H^{\prime\prime})}

\displaystyle=\bar{\gamma}^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}\cup\big(\bar{\gamma}^{(H,H^{\prime})}\setminus\bar{\xi}^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}\big)\,,

\displaystyle\bar{\xi}^{(H,H^{\prime\prime})}

\displaystyle=\bar{\xi}^{(H,H^{\prime})}\cup\big(\bar{\xi}^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}\setminus\bar{\gamma}^{(H,H^{\prime})}\big)\,.

Observe that, after the first gluing operation is performed, the marks
\bar{\gamma}
and
\bar{\xi}
remain geodesic, as observed in Section
3.3
.
Note also that the order of the gluings in (
46
) is not important, due to (
12
).

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Proposition
26
, and we
only sketch the argument. Again, we view
I^{(H,H^{\prime\prime})}
as a disjoint
union
I^{(H,H^{\prime})}\sqcup I^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}
(denoting elements of these
sets with superscripts
0
,
1
respectively) where the extremities

T_{H^{\prime}}^{0}
,
T_{H^{\prime}}^{1}
and
\bar{T}_{H^{\prime}}^{0}
,
\bar{T}_{H^{\prime}}^{1}
are
identified. We then observe that the pseudometric
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mud\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{g}
can be
viewed as a quotient
d/R_{1}
, where
d
is the disjoint union metric on

I^{(H,H^{\prime})}\sqcup I^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}
whose restriction to each interval
composing this set
equals the restriction of
d_{g}
to that interval, and
R_{1}
is the
coarsest equivalence relation containing

\big\{\big(\Xi_{T_{H^{\prime}}}(r)^{0},\Gamma_{T_{H^{\prime}}}(r)^{1}\big),\ 0\leq r\leq g(T_{H^{\prime}})-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mug\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(T_{H},T_{H^{\prime}})\vee\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mug\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(T_{H^{\prime}},T_{H^{\prime\prime}})\big\}\\
\text{ and }\big\{\big(\Gamma_{\bar{T}_{H^{\prime}}}(r)^{0},\Xi_{\bar{T}_{H^{\prime}}}(r)^{1}\big),\ 0\leq r\leq g(\bar{T}_{H^{\prime}})-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mug\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\bar{T}_{H},\bar{T}_{H^{\prime}})\vee\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mug\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\bar{T}_{H^{\prime}},\bar{T}_{H^{\prime\prime}})\big\}.

Moreover, the equivalence relation
\{d_{f}=0\}
factorizes in the sense
that, if
d_{f}(s,t)=0
with
s
,
t\in I^{(H,H^{\prime\prime})}
, then it must hold that

s
,
t
belong either both to
I^{(H,H^{\prime})}
or both to

I^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}
. Therefore, setting
R_{2}
as the equivalence
relation on
I^{(H,H^{\prime})}\sqcup I^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}
defined by
s^{i}\mathbin{R}_{2}t^{j}
if
and only if
d_{f}(s,t)=0
and
i=j\in\{0,1\}
, we obtain

\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(H,H^{\prime\prime})}=(d/R_{1})/R_{2}=(d/R_{2})/R_{1}\,.

We now recognize that
d/R_{2}
is the pseudometric of the disjoint
union of
(I^{(H,H^{\prime})},\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(H,H^{\prime})})
and

(I^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})},\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})})
, while
R_{1}
can be seen as the
coarsest equivalence relation obtained by first gluing
\xi^{(H,H^{\prime})}

with
\gamma^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}
, and then
\bar{\gamma}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
with

\bar{\xi}^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}
.
∎

Due to the fact that the second gluing operation in Proposition
39
involves two geodesics belonging to the same
space, there is no direct analog of Corollary
27
. However, we have the following
alternative, which is an immediate consequence of Lemma
21
and a crude estimate of the length of the
path
\bar{\xi}^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}
.

Proposition 40
.

Under the same assumptions as in Proposition
39
,
it holds that for every
s
,
t\in I^{(H,H^{\prime})}
,

\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(H,H^{\prime\prime})}(s,t)\leq\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(H,H^{\prime})}(s,t)\leq\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(H,H^{\prime\prime})}(s,t)+\omega(g;I^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})})\,.

Finally, we observe that the metric space
(M_{f,g},D_{f,g})

obtained by metric gluing of the pseudometric
d_{g}
along the
relation
\{d_{f}=0\}
, rather than using
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mud\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{g}
as in the
definition of
\mathsf{Qd}_{f,g}
, is related to the latter by a final gluing
operation. The proof is analog to that of Proposition
39
, noting
that
d_{g}
is the gluing of
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mud\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{g}
along the coarsest equivalence
relation containing
\{(\Gamma_{0}(r),\Xi_{0}(r)),r\geq 0\}
.

Lemma 41
.

One has
\big(M_{f,g},D_{f,g}\big)=G\big(\mathsf{Qd}_{f,g};\gamma,\bar{\xi}\big)
.

6.2
Random continuum quadrilaterals

Let us now describe the limiting continuum quadrilaterals that appear in Theorem
14
, by suitably
randomizing the quadrilateral trajectory
(f,g)
. We let
(X,W)
be the canonical
process defined on quadrilateral trajectories. We introduce, for any process
Y
defined on an interval containing
0
, the piece of notation
\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muY\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muY\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(0\wedge t,0\vee t)
.

Let us fix
A
,
\bar{A}
,
H\in(0,\infty)
and

\Delta\in\mathbb{R}
. We let
\mathbf{Quad}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}
be the probability
distribution under which

•

(X_{t},0\leq t\leq A)
and
(X_{-t},0\leq t\leq\bar{A})
are
independent first-passage bridges of standard Brownian motion from
0
to
-H
, with durations
A
and
\bar{A}
;

•

conditionally given
X
, the process
W
has same law as
(Z_{t}+\zeta_{-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}},-\bar{A}\leq t\leq A)
, where
Z
is the
random snake driven by
X-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu
, and
\zeta
is a standard Brownian bridge of duration
H
and terminal value
\Delta
, independent of
X
and
Z
.

In this way, the probability distribution
\mathbf{Quad}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}
is carried by quadrilateral
trajectories on the interval
[-\bar{A},A]
. We remark that, in fact, we
can view
W
more directly as the random snake driven by
X
,
conditioned on the event
\{W_{A}=\Delta\}
, a fact that we leave to the
interested reader.

Definition 42
.

The
quadrilateral with half-areas
A
,
\bar{A}
,
width
H
and tilt
\Delta
, generically denote by
\mathsf{Qd}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}
, is the
6
-marked
1
-measured metric space

\mathsf{Qd}_{X,W}
under the law
\mathbf{Quad}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}
, endowed with its area measure
\mu
, as well as the marking

\partial\mathsf{Qd}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}=\big(\gamma,\xi,\bar{\gamma},\bar{\xi}\big)\,,

where
\gamma
,
\bar{\gamma}
are geodesic marks as usual, while
\xi
,
\bar{\xi}
are seen as (nonoriented) geodesic segments, that is, given without their origins.

As for slices, the piece of notation
\partial\mathsf{Qd}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}
comes from the result of Lemma
25
: the boundary of the topological disk
\mathsf{Qd}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}
is the union of
\gamma
,
\xi
,
\bar{\gamma}
,
\bar{\xi}
, which intersect only at
the points
\gamma(0)=\bar{\xi}(0)=\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbf{p}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{f,g}(0)
,
x_{*}
,

\bar{\gamma}(0)=\xi(0)=\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbf{p}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{f,g}(A)=\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbf{p}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{f,g}(-\bar{A})
, and
\bar{x}_{*}
.

6.3
The Brownian plane, and its embedded quadrilaterals

Similarly to the fact that (free) slices can be found in
the Brownian half-plane, one can obtain quadrilaterals from the Brownian plane, as we now explain. We let
\mathbf{Plane}
be the probability distribution on
\mathcal{C}^{2}
under which

•

the process
X
is a two-sided standard Brownian motion
13
13

13

This means that
(X_{t},t\geq 0)
and
(X_{-t},t\geq 0)
are independent standard
Brownian motions.
, and

•

the process
W
is the random snake driven by
X
.

The measure
\mathbf{Plane}
is carried by quadrilateral trajectories defined over
\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 43
.

The
Brownian plane
, generically denoted by
\mathsf{BP}
, is the metric space
(M_{X,W},D_{X,W})
defined by (
25
), considered under
\mathbf{Plane}
. Letting
\mathbf{p}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathsf{BP}
be the canonical projection,
it is endowed with the area
measure
\mu=\mathbf{p}_{*}\mathrm{Leb}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

In this definition, beware that the metric is indeed defined by (
25
) rather than (
45
), which would produce the
metric space
\mathsf{Qd}_{X,W}=\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,\infty)}=(\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muM\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{X,W},\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{X,W})
. Observe
that, by Lemma
41
,

\mathsf{BP}=G\big(\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,\infty)};\gamma^{(0,\infty)},\bar{\xi}^{(0,\infty)}\big)\,;

(47)

see Figure
15
below for an illustration. Alternatively, the space

\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,\infty)}
can be seen as cutting the Brownian plane along the
geodesic ray
\gamma_{0}=\xi_{0}
; we do not go into further details as we will not explicitly need this property.

Note also that, despite the similarity between this definition and that of
the Brownian half-plane, there is no marking now because, as its name suggests, the
Brownian plane is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and therefore has an empty boundary
as a topological surface.

One should finally mind that this definition is different from the original one given in
[
CLG14
]
, which will be recalled in Appendix
A
; in a nutshell, one goes from a definition to the other by changing
X
into the process obtained by taking its Pitman transform both on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and on
\mathbb{R}_{-}
.

Free quadrilaterals.

Similarly to the discussion of Section
5.3
, the Brownian
plane satisfies a Markov property which can be interpreted as a
“flow” of continuum quadrilaterals. Fixing
0\leq H\leq H^{\prime}\leq\infty
, and
denoting by

\vartheta_{H}:t\in[\bar{T}_{H^{\prime}}-\bar{T}_{H},T_{H^{\prime}}-T_{H}]\mapsto(t+\bar{T}_{H})\mathbf{1}_{t<0}+(t+T_{H})\mathbf{1}_{t\geq 0}\,,

we see that the process

\big(X^{(H,H^{\prime})}\circ\vartheta_{H}+H,W^{(H,H^{\prime})}\circ\vartheta_{H}-W_{T_{H}}\big)
is
independent of
(X^{(0,H)},W^{(0,H)})
,

(X^{(H^{\prime},+\infty)},W^{(H^{\prime},+\infty)})
, and has same distribution as

(X^{(H^{\prime}-H)},W^{(H^{\prime}-H)})
. This can be proved by excursion
theory of
(X,W)
separately in positive and negative times; we
omit the details, which are similar to those presented in Section
5.3
.

Under
\mathbf{Plane}
, the process

(X^{(0,H)})
is a two-sided Brownian motion killed at its first
hitting times
T_{H}
,
\bar{T}_{H}
of
-H
respectively in positive and negative
times, while the process
\big(W^{(0,H)}_{T_{x}},0\leq x\leq H\big)
is a
standard Brownian motion killed at time
H
. This implies that the law
of
(X^{(0,H)},W^{(0,H)})
under
\mathbf{Plane}
equals

\mathbf{FQuad}_{H}=\int_{(0,\infty)^{2}}q_{H}(A)q_{H}(\bar{A})\,\mathrm{d}A\,\mathrm{d}\bar{A}\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{H}(\Delta)\,\mathrm{d}\Delta\,\mathbf{Quad}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}\,,

where the densities
p_{t}
,
q_{x}
are defined after (
30
). A
random metric space with same law as
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}
under
\mathbf{FQuad}_{H}

will be referred to as a
free (continuum) quadrilateral
of width
H
. From these considerations and Proposition
39
, we
obtain the following result.

Proposition 44
.

Let
0\leq H<H^{\prime}<H^{\prime\prime}\leq\infty
. Then, under
\mathbf{Plane}
, it holds that

\mathsf{Qd}^{(H,H^{\prime\prime})}=G\big(G\big(\mathsf{Qd}^{(H,H^{\prime})},\mathsf{Qd}^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})};\xi^{(H,H^{\prime})},\gamma^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}\big);\bar{\gamma}^{(H,H^{\prime})},\bar{\xi}^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}\big)\,,

where the glued spaces
\mathsf{Qd}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
and
\mathsf{Qd}^{(H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime})}
are
independent. Moreover,
\mathsf{Qd}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
is a free continuum
quadrilateral of width
H^{\prime}-H
.

Figure 15:
Seeing free quadrilaterals in the Brownian plane. The
union of the dark yellow regions forms
\mathsf{Qd}^{(H,H^{\prime\prime})}
. The
dotted brown line is
\{\mathbf{p}(T_{h})\,:\,h\geq 0\}
. Note how
\mathsf{BP}
itself is obtained by gluing
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,\infty)}
along
the geodesics
\gamma^{(0,\infty)}
and
\xi^{(0,\infty)}
, resulting
in the geodesic
\gamma_{0}=\xi_{0}
.

We refer to Figure
15
for an illustration, which suggests, as is
proved in the following proposition, that
quadrilaterals are topological disks bounded by their geodesic sides. In contrast with our treatment of slices, a difficulty
arises from the fact that the quadrilaterals
\mathsf{Qd}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
are not isometrically
embedded in
\mathsf{BP}
, and, in general, not even locally isometrically embedded (think
of a point of
\mathsf{BP}
lying on the geodesic
\gamma_{0}
).

Proposition 45
.

For every
H\in(0,\infty)
, almost surely under
\mathbf{FQuad}_{H}
, the
quadrilateral
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}
is a topological disk with boundary
given by the geodesics
\gamma^{(0,H)}
,
\xi^{(0,H)}
,

\bar{\gamma}^{(0,H)}
and
\bar{\xi}^{(0,H)}
, which pairwise meet only
at the points
\gamma(0)=\bar{\xi}(0)
,
\xi(0)=\bar{\gamma}(0)
, and the
apexes
x_{\smash{*}}^{(0,H)}
and
\bar{x}_{\smash{*}}^{(0,H)}
.

In order to prove this proposition and for later use, it will be
important to characterize the set
\{D_{X,W}=0\}
.

Lemma 46
.

The following holds almost surely under
\mathbf{Plane}
. For every
s
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
such that
s\neq t
, it holds that

D_{X,W}(s,t)=0
if and only if either
d_{X}(s,t)=0
or
d_{W}(s,t)=0
, these
two cases being mutually exclusive.

Proof.

By
[
CLG14
, Proposition 11]
, it holds that
D_{X,W}(s,t)=0

implies that
d_{X}(s,t)=0
or
d_{W}(s,t)=0
. The fact that these two
properties are mutually exclusive is a consequence of the fact from
Lemma 2.2 in
[
LG07
]
that
almost surely,
if
s
is a point such that
X_{u}\geq X_{s}
for every
u\in[s,s+\varepsilon]
for some
\varepsilon>0
, then it must hold that
\inf_{u\in[s,s+\delta]}W_{u}<W_{s}
for every
\delta\in(0,\varepsilon)
. In fact,

[
LG07
, Lemma 2.2]
is proved when the process
X
is distributed as a standard Brownian excursion, and
W
as a random snake
Z

driven by this excursion. However, being a local property of the
processes at hand, it extends easily to our setting by an absolute
continuity argument. Details are left to the reader.
∎

To the terminology of Section
5.1.1
, we add the piece of notation
\llbracket a,b\mathclose{\llbracket}_{f}=\llbracket a,b\rrbracket_{f}\setminus\{b\}
for
a
,
b\in\mathcal{T}_{f}
. The important consequence of this lemma for our purposes is the following. Almost surely, if

a
,
b\in\mathcal{T}_{X}
and
c
,
d\in\mathcal{T}_{W}
, then the paths
\pi_{X}(\llbracket a,b\rrbracket_{X})

and
\pi_{W}(\llbracket c,d\rrbracket_{W})
are simple paths. Furthermore,
\pi_{X}(\llbracket a,b\mathclose{\llbracket}_{X})
may intersect

\pi_{W}(\llbracket c,d\mathclose{\llbracket}_{W})
only if
\pi_{X}(a)=\pi_{W}(c)
, in which case these paths intersect
at this point only. In particular, if we denote the geodesic ray

\mathbf{p}_{X}(\{s\geq t:X_{s}=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(t,s)\})
of
\mathcal{T}_{X}
by
\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{X}(t),\infty\mathclose{\llbracket}_{X}
, then

\pi_{X}(\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{X}(t),\infty\mathclose{\llbracket}_{X})
is a simple path in
\mathsf{BP}
. For instance, in Figure
15
, we represented the simple path
\pi_{X}(\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{X}(0),\infty\mathclose{\llbracket}_{X})
with a dotted brown line.

Proof of Proposition
45
.

Let us depart slightly from the setting of the statement and fix for now
two numbers
0\leq H<H^{\prime}<\infty
.

Claim
.

We assume that the geodesics

\gamma^{(H,H^{\prime})}
and
\bar{\xi}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
do not intersect

\gamma_{0}
in
\mathsf{BP}
. Then the following holds.

(
i
)

The geodesics
\gamma^{(H,H^{\prime})}
,
\xi^{(H,H^{\prime})}
,
\bar{\gamma}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
,
\bar{\xi}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
intersect only at the points

x_{\smash{*}}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
,
\bar{\gamma}^{(H,H^{\prime})}(0)=\xi^{(H,H^{\prime})}(0)
,
\bar{x}_{\smash{*}}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
and
\gamma^{(H,H^{\prime})}(0)=\bar{\xi}^{(H,H^{\prime})}(0)

in this cyclic order, and their union forms a Jordan curve
C
.

(
ii
)

The set

\mathbf{p}(I^{(H,H^{\prime})})\subseteq\mathsf{BP}
is the closure of the bounded connected
component of
\mathsf{BP}\setminus C
.

Indeed, note that
\mathbf{p}_{X}(T_{H})
and
\mathbf{p}_{X}(T_{H^{\prime}})
are two distinct points of
\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{X}(0),\infty\mathclose{\llbracket}_{X}
, so
that their images by
\pi_{X}
are distinct in
\mathsf{BP}
. Then the paths
\gamma^{(H,H^{\prime})}
and

\xi^{(H,H^{\prime})}
are the images by
\pi_{W}
of the two geodesic paths

\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{W}(T_{H}),a_{*}(W^{(H,H^{\prime})})\rrbracket_{W}
and
\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{W}(T_{H^{\prime}}),a_{*}(W^{(H,H^{\prime})})\rrbracket_{W}
in
\mathcal{T}_{W}
,
which by definition meet only at
a_{*}(W^{(H,H^{\prime})})
, and their union is
the geodesic
\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{W}(T_{H}),\mathbf{p}_{W}(T_{H^{\prime}})\rrbracket_{W}
in
\mathcal{T}_{W}
, which is thus projected
via
\pi_{W}
to a simple path in
\mathsf{BP}
. Therefore,
\gamma^{(H,H^{\prime})}
and
\xi^{(H,H^{\prime})}
meet only at

x_{\smash{*}}^{(H,H^{\prime})}=\pi_{W}(a_{*}(W^{(H,H^{\prime})}))
.
The same reasoning shows that
\bar{\gamma}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
and

\bar{\xi}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
intersect only at
\bar{x}_{\smash{*}}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
, and gives that
the points
x_{\smash{*}}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
and
\bar{x}_{\smash{*}}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
are distinct points
(because they are distinct points in
\mathcal{T}_{W}
lying inside two
geodesics).

Next, if the path
\gamma^{(H,H^{\prime})}
does not intersect
\gamma_{0}=\pi_{W}(\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{W}(0),\infty\mathclose{\llbracket}_{W})
, then necessarily the path

\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{W}(T_{H}),a_{*}(W^{(H,H^{\prime})})\rrbracket_{W}
must be disjoint from

\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{W}(0),\infty\mathclose{\llbracket}_{W}
, which means that

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(T_{H},T_{H^{\prime}})>\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(0,T_{H})\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\bar{T}_{H^{\prime}},\bar{T}_{H})>\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\bar{T}_{H},0)\,.

This implies that
\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{W}(T_{H^{\prime}}),a_{*}(W^{(H,H^{\prime})})\mathclose{\llbracket}_{W}
is also
disjoint from
\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{W}(0),\infty\mathclose{\llbracket}_{W}
, and by projecting by
\pi_{W}
,
that
\xi^{(H,H^{\prime})}
is disjoint from
\gamma_{0}
. A similar
argument applies to
\bar{\gamma}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
and

\bar{\xi}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
. Therefore, under the conditions of the claim, the
paths
\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{W}(T_{H}),a_{*}(W^{(H,H^{\prime})})\rrbracket_{W}
and
\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{W}(\bar{T}_{H}),\bar{a}_{*}(W^{(H,H^{\prime})})\rrbracket_{W}
are disjoint paths in
\mathcal{T}_{W}
, and their
projections
\gamma^{(H,H^{\prime})}
and
\bar{\xi}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
via
\pi_{W}

intersect, if at all, only at their
extremities. It is indeed the case that
\mathbf{p}(T_{H})=\mathbf{p}(\bar{T}_{H})
, while, as we already saw,
x_{\smash{*}}^{(H,H^{\prime})}\neq\bar{x}_{\smash{*}}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
. This proves
(i)
.

The argument for
(ii)
is similar to that in the proof of
Lemma
31
, where the role of the base is now played by the infinite path
\pi_{X}(\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{X}(0),\infty\mathclose{\llbracket}_{X})=\{\mathbf{p}(T_{h})\,:\,h\geq 0\}
. For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we let

\Sigma_{t}(r)=\inf\{s\geq t:X_{s}=X_{t}-r\}\qquad\text{ for }\qquad 0\leq r\leq X_{t}-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}\,,

where we recall that
\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(0\wedge t,0\vee t)
. The range of
\mathbf{p}_{X}\circ\Sigma_{t}
is the geodesic path

\llbracket\mathbf{p}_{X}(t),\mathbf{p}_{X}(T_{-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}})\rrbracket_{X}
in
\mathcal{T}_{X}
and its image by
\pi_{X}

defines a path
\sigma_{t}=\mathbf{p}\circ\Sigma_{t}
from
\mathbf{p}(t)
to
\mathbf{p}(T_{-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}})
. Moreover, by Lemma
46
, the paths
\sigma_{t}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, do not intersect any of the geodesics
\gamma_{s}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
, except
possibly at their starting points. There are now the following possibilities.

•

If
t\in I^{(H,H^{\prime})}
, then
\sigma_{t}
ends on the path
(\mathbf{p}(T_{h}),H\leq h\leq H^{\prime})
. This means that, if
\mathbf{p}(t)
does not belong to the four geodesics
\gamma^{(H,H^{\prime})}
,
\xi^{(H,H^{\prime})}
,
\bar{\gamma}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
,
\bar{\xi}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
, then we may connect it to, say, the point
\mathbf{p}(T_{(H+H^{\prime})/2})
of the bounded set
\mathsf{Qd}^{(H,H^{\prime})}
, without crossing the four mentioned geodesics.

•

If
t\notin I^{(H,H^{\prime})}
, we distinguish two cases.

–

If
t\notin[\bar{T}_{H^{\prime}},T_{H^{\prime}}]
, then
\sigma_{t}
ends on the unbounded path
\{\mathbf{p}(T_{h}):h>H\}
.

–

If
t\in(\bar{T}_{H},T_{H})
, then
\sigma_{t}
ends on
\{\mathbf{p}(T_{h}):0\leq h<H\}
.

If
\mathbf{p}(t)
does not belong to the four geodesics of interest, then it may be joined without crossing the four geodesics either to the unbounded path
\{\mathbf{p}(T_{h}):h>H\}
or to the unbounded path
\{\mathbf{p}(T_{h}):0\leq h<H\}\cup\gamma_{0}
, by the assumption that
\gamma_{0}
does not intersect the four geodesics.

This completes the proof of the claim.

Now fix
H>0
, and consider another positive number
H_{0}
to be
thought of as large. Since
we know that
\mathsf{Qd}^{(H_{0},H_{0}+H)}
under
\mathbf{Plane}
has same
distribution as
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}
, we may work with the former space
rather than with the latter. For every
\varepsilon>0
it holds that there
exists some
H_{0}
large enough such that with probability at least

1-\varepsilon
,
the geodesics
\gamma^{(H_{0},H_{0}+H)}
and

\bar{\xi}^{(H_{0},H_{0}+H)}
do not intersect
\gamma_{0}
. Indeed, this happens whenever

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(T_{H_{0}},T_{H_{0}+H})>\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(0,T_{H_{0}})
or, equivalently,

W_{T_{H_{0}}}-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(0,T_{H_{0}})>W_{T_{H_{0}}}-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(T_{H_{0}},T_{H_{0}+H})\,,

(48)

and similarly in negative times. The two sides of (
48
) are independent by the
Markov property stated above; the right-hand side has a
distribution that depends only on
H
, while the left-hand side,
which has same distribution as
-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muW\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(0,T_{H_{0}})
by a simple time-reversal argument,
converges to
\infty
in probability as
H_{0}\to\infty
.

By the claim, we obtain that on an event happening with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
, the set

\mathbf{p}(I^{(H_{0},H_{0}+H)})
is the closure of the connected component of
the complement in
\mathsf{BP}
of the paths

\gamma^{(H_{0},H_{0}+H)},\quad\xi^{(H_{0},H_{0}+H)},\quad\bar{\gamma}^{(H_{0},H_{0}+H)},\quad\bar{\xi}^{(H_{0},H_{0}+H)}\,,

which all together form a Jordan curve. On this event, the
identity mapping on
I^{(H_{0},H_{0}+H)}
induces, by precomposition
with the projection mappings
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{p}^{(H_{0},H_{0}+H)}
, a bijective
mapping
\phi
from the compact space
\mathsf{Qd}^{(H_{0},H_{0}+H)}
to

\mathbf{p}(I^{(H_{0},H_{0}+H)})
, which is
1
-Lipschitz since

D_{X,W}\leq\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(H_{0},H_{0}+H)}
by Lemma
21
, (
47
) and Proposition
44
. This shows that
\phi
is a
homeomorphism, and therefore, with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
, the
space
\mathsf{Qd}^{(H_{0},H_{0}+H)}
has the properties claimed in the
statement. Using the fact that
\mathsf{Qd}^{(H_{0},H_{0}+H)}
has same
distribution as
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}
and that
\varepsilon
was arbitrary, we conclude.
∎

The continuum quadrilaterals of the preceding section can be linked to
the free quadrilaterals embedded in the Brownian plane by an absolute
continuity argument, whose proof is similar to that of Lemma
32
and is omitted.

Lemma 47
.

Fix
0<K<H
, as well as
A>0
,
\bar{A}>0
, and
\Delta\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, for every
nonnegative function
G
that is measurable with respect to the

\sigma
-algebra generated by
(X^{(0,K)},W^{(0,K)})
, one has

\mathbf{Quad}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}[G]=\mathbf{Plane}\big[\psi_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}(T_{K},-\bar{T}_{K},K,W_{T_{K}})\cdot G\big]\,,

where

\psi_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}(A^{\prime},\bar{A}^{\prime},H^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})=\frac{q_{H-H^{\prime}}(A-A^{\prime})}{q_{H}(A)}\frac{q_{H-H^{\prime}}(\bar{A}-\bar{A}^{\prime})}{q_{H}(\bar{A})}\frac{p_{H-H^{\prime}}(\Delta-\Delta^{\prime})}{p_{H}(\Delta)}\,.

This allows to obtain, as stated in Lemma
25
, the topology of quadrilaterals.

Proof of Lemma
25
for quadrilaterals.

The proof is similar to that for slices. We use the fact
that the Brownian plane is topologically a plane

[
CLG14
, Proposition 13]
, as well
as
[
CLG14
, Proposition 4]
to obtain that it is locally of
Hausdorff dimension
4
from the analog result about the Brownian
sphere
[
LG06
]
. We deduce from there the desired
properties for a free quadrilateral.
To extend this result to quadrilaterals
\mathsf{Qd}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}
,
which we view as
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}
under the law
\mathbf{Quad}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}
, we use the fact from Proposition
39
that it
can be seen as the gluing of
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H/2)}
and
\mathsf{Qd}^{(H/2,H)}
along
the boundaries
\xi^{(0,H/2)}
and
\gamma^{(H/2,H)}
on the one hand,
and
\bar{\gamma}^{(0,H/2)}
and
\bar{\xi}^{(H/2,H)}
on the other
hand. By the absolute continuity relation stated in Lemma
47
, we see that the law of
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H/2)}
is
absolutely continuous with respect to that of a free quadrilateral
with width
H/2
, and the same is true for
\mathsf{Qd}^{(H/2,H)}
. Using
Proposition
45
, we obtain that

\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}
is obtained by gluing two topological disks, both
locally of Hausdorff dimension 4, along part
of their boundaries, which allows to conclude.
∎

6.4
The uniform infinite planar quadrangulation

The UIPQ is the whole plane pendant of the UIHPQ defined in
Section
5.4
. It is simpler to describe and was introduced
earlier
[
CD06
,
Kri05
,
CMM13
]
. Let

(\mathbf{T}^{k},k\in\mathbb{Z})
be a two-sided sequence
of independent Bienaymé–Galton–Watson trees with a geometric offspring
distribution of parameter
1/2
. We construct an infinite tree
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
embedded in the plane by mapping the roots of
\mathbf{T}^{k}
and of
\mathbf{T}^{-k}
to the point
\rho^{k}=(k,0)
for every
k\geq 0
, in such a way
that, except for these roots, the trees
\mathbf{T}^{k}
,
k\geq 0
are embedded in the
open upper half-plane and the trees
\mathbf{T}^{k}
,
k<0
are embedded in the
open lower half-plane, without intersection. Lastly, we link the roots
\rho^{k}
,
\rho^{k+1}
with a horizontal segment for every
k\geq 0
.

Conditionally on
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
, we assign to the edges random numbers, independent and uniformly distributed in
\{-1,0,1\}
, and let
\lambda_{\infty}:V(\mathbf{T}_{\infty})\to\mathbb{Z}
be the labeling function whose increments along the edges are given by these numbers. Note that this uniquely defines
\lambda_{\infty}
, up to the usual addition of a constant. We call
(\mathbf{T}_{\infty},\lambda_{\infty})
the
infinite random well-labeled tree
. We then let

(c_{i},i\in\mathbb{Z})
be the sequence of corners of
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
in contour
order, with origin the corner
c_{0}
corresponding to the root
of
\mathbf{T}^{0}
. The
uniform infinite planar quadrangulation
(
UIPQ
for short) is then the infinite map
Q_{\infty}
obtained by applying the CVS
construction to
(\mathbf{T}_{\infty},\lambda_{\infty})
, that is, by linking
every corner to its successor as defined in
Section
2.1
, and removing all edges of the
tree afterward. The root of
Q_{\infty}
is defined as the corner
preceding the arc from
c_{0}
to its successor. As with the UIHPQ,
there is no need to add an extra vertex with a corner
c_{\infty}
.

Similarly as before, we denote by
v_{i}
the vertex of
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
incident to
c_{i}
and by
\Upsilon(i)\in\mathbb{Z}
the index of the tree to which
v_{i}
belongs. We then define the
contour
and
label processes
on
\mathbb{R}
by

C(i)=d_{\mathbf{T}^{\Upsilon(i)}}\big(v_{i},\rho^{|\Upsilon(i)|}\big)-|\Upsilon(i)|\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Lambda(i)=\lambda_{\infty}(v_{i})-\lambda_{\infty}(v_{0})\,,\qquad i\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

and by linear interpolation between integer values; see Figure
16
. Observe that, in contrast with the definition of Section
5.4
for an infinite forest, there is an absolute value in the definition of
C
. In fact, changing the
-
into a
+
amounts to taking the so-called
Pitman transform
, which is a one-to-one mapping, so this is just a matter of convention. We will come back to this in Appendix
A
. We can easily check that
C
is distributed as a two-sided random walk conditioned
14
14

14

See Remark
48
for the explanation of this conditioning.
on
C(-1)=-1
.

Figure 16:
Contour and label processes associated with

(\mathbf{T}_{\infty},\lambda_{\infty})
. The infinite dashed line is the so-called
spine
of the tree. The tree
\mathbf{T}^{0}
and the corresponding encoding processes are highlighted. Similarly as on Figure
14
, one might see the contour process as recording the height of a particle moving at speed one around the infinite tree obtained by now letting
\rho^{k}
be located at
(0,-k)
with
\mathbf{T}^{k}
grafted on its right and
\mathbf{T}^{-k}
on its left (both upright), for
k\geq 0
; see the left of Figure
18
for an illustration.

As before, we extend
C
and
\Lambda
to functions on
\mathbb{R}
by linear interpolation between integer values. For
k\geq 0
, we set

\bar{\tau}_{k}=\max\big\{i\leq 0\,:\,C(i)=-k\big\}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\tau_{k}=\min\big\{i\geq 0\,:\,C(i)=-k\big\}\,.

Note that, for a fixed
k\geq 0
, the process
(k+C(s+\tau_{k}),0\leq s\leq\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k})

is the contour process of
\mathbf{T}^{k}
, while, for
k\geq 1
,
(k+C(s+\bar{\tau}_{k+1}+1),0\leq s\leq\bar{\tau}_{k}-\bar{\tau}_{k+1}-1)
is the contour
process of
\mathbf{T}^{-k}
without the last descending step. Therefore, in
this notation, the forest composed of the
k
leftmost trees in the
upper half-plane is coded by the interval
[0,\tau_{k}]
,
while the forest composed of the
k
leftmost trees in the lower
half-plane is coded by the interval
[\bar{\tau}_{k+1}+1,0]
. This slightly annoying shift will appear later on,
in particular in the statement of Lemma
56
.

Remark 48
.

As with the UIHPQ, the above definition gives a slight
variant of the usual UIPQ, which is similarly defined by
adding a further tree rooted at
\rho^{0}
embedded in the lower
half-plane, or equivalently, by removing
the conditioning by
\{C(-1)=-1\}
. This bias is similar to the one we had for the UIHPQ. Here again, the reason for using this definition is that it will give the natural semigroup property for the discrete quadrilaterals.

We set

D_{\infty}(i,j)=d_{Q_{\infty}}(v_{i},v_{j})\,,\qquad i,j\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

(49)

and extend it to a function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by bilinear
interpolation between integer values, as in (
33
). We
define the renormalized versions
C_{(n)}
,
\Lambda_{(n)}
,
D_{(n)}

of
C
,
\Lambda
,
D_{\infty}
by (
34
). The following proposition builds on the convergence obtained in
[
CLG14
]
of the UIPQ to the Brownian plane. As was the case for the UIHPQ, it does not appear in this exact form in
[
CLG14
]
and calls for a proof, which is postponed to Appendix
A
. Recall from Section
6.3
the definition of the distribution
\mathbf{Plane}
.

Proposition 49
.

The following convergence in distribution holds on
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{(2)}
:

\big(C_{(n)},\Lambda_{(n)},D_{(n)}\big)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits_{n\to\infty}^{(d)}}\big(X,W,D_{X,W}\big)\,,

where the limiting triple is understood under
\mathbf{Plane}
.

6.5
Discrete quadrilaterals in the UIPQ

We proceed as in the last paragraph of Section
5.4
. But, here, the lack of an analog of Corollary
27
makes matter substantially more intricate. We consider a sequence
(h_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\frac{h_{n}}{\sqrt{2n}}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits_{n\to\infty}}H>0\,.

For each
n
, we let
F_{n}
be the random forest consisting of the
h_{n}
trees
\mathbf{T}^{0}
,
\mathbf{T}^{1}
, …,
\mathbf{T}^{h_{n}-1}
, and
\rho^{h_{n}}
, as well as
\bar{F}_{n}
be the random forest consisting of the
h_{n}
trees
\mathbf{T}^{-h_{n}}
,
\mathbf{T}^{-h_{n}+1}
, …,
\mathbf{T}^{-1}
and
\rho^{0}
. The pair
(F_{n},\bar{F}_{n})
is a double forest in the terminology of Section
2.4
and the map
F_{n}\cup\bar{F}_{n}
is well labeled by the restriction of
\lambda_{\infty}
. We denote by
Q_{n}
the corresponding quadrilateral and by
v_{*}
,
\bar{v}_{*}
its apexes; similarly to the previous section, we see it as part of the UIPQ
Q_{\infty}
.

For each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the vertex
v_{i}
of
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
incident to
c_{i}
can still be seen as a vertex of
Q_{n}
when
\bar{\tau}_{h_{n}+1}+1\leq i\leq\tau_{h_{n}}
. We set

\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{n}(i,j)=d_{Q_{n}}(v_{i},v_{j})\,,\qquad\bar{\tau}_{h_{n}+1}+1\leq i,j\leq\tau_{h_{n}}\,,

extend it to a function on
[\bar{\tau}_{h_{n}+1}+1,\tau_{h_{n}}]^{2}
by bilinear interpolation between integer values as in (
33
), and define its renormalized version

\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{(n)}(s,t)=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{n}(2ns,2nt)}{(8n/9)^{1/4}}\,,\qquad\frac{\bar{\tau}_{h_{n}+1}+1}{2n}\leq s,t\leq\frac{\tau_{h_{n}}}{2n}\,.

(50)

This section is devoted to the proof of the following result, which essentially amounts to stating that, jointly with the convergence of
\Omega_{n}(Q_{\infty})
to the Brownian plane, the properly rescaled quadrilateral
\Omega_{n}(Q_{n})
converges to
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}
.

Theorem 50
.

The following convergence in distribution holds in
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{(2)}\times\mathcal{C}^{(2)}
:

\big(C_{(n)},\Lambda_{(n)},D_{(n)},\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{(n)}\big)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits_{n\to\infty}^{(d)}}\big(X,W,D_{X,W},\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(0,H)}\big)\,,

where the limiting quadruple is understood under
\mathbf{Plane}
.

The first step in the proof is the following tightness statement.

Lemma 51
.

From every increasing sequence of integers, one may extract a
subsequence along which the following convergence holds in
\mathcal{C}^{(2)}
, jointly with the convergence of Proposition
49
:

\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{(n)}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits_{n\to\infty}^{(d)}}\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\,,

(51)

where
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
is a random pseudometric on
[\bar{T}_{H},T_{H}]
.

Proof.

The classical tightness argument from
[
LG07
, Proposition 3.2]
implies
that the laws of
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{(n)}
,
n\geq 1
, are
tight in
\mathcal{C}^{(2)}
. Together with Proposition
49
, this yields the tightness
of the laws of the sequence of the quadruples

(C_{(n)},\Lambda_{(n)},D_{(n)},\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{(n)})
, and therefore, by
Prokhorov’s theorem, their joint convergence in distribution, at least
along some subsequence, to a limiting process
(X,W,D_{X,W},\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
,
where the law of the first three components is determined by
Proposition
49
.
Since
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{(n)}
is a
pseudometric on
[(\bar{\tau}_{h_{n}+1}+1)/2n,\tau_{h_{n}}/2n]
, and because
of the convergence of
C_{(n)}
to
X
implying the joint convergence
of the bounds of this interval to
\bar{T}_{H}
,
T_{H}
,
it is straightforward to check that all
subsequential limits of these laws are carried by functions that are
pseudometrics on the interval
[\bar{T}_{H},T_{H}]
.
∎

From now on, we fix
a subsequence along which (
51
) holds, and only consider
for the time being values of
n
that belong to this particular
subsequence. By the Skorokhod representation theorem, we may and will
assume that this convergence furthermore holds almost surely.

We define
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathsf{Qd}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
as the set
[\bar{T}_{H},T_{H}]/\{\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=0\}
, endowed
with the metric
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
. Beware that it is not clear
at all that
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathsf{Qd}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}
, and
this is precisely what we want to prove. More precisely, we aim at
showing that, almost surely, for every
s
,
t\in[\bar{T}_{H},T_{H}]
, it
holds that

\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)=\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(0,H)}(s,t)
, which will entail Theorem
50
.

Since the real number
H
is fixed once and for all, we will use in the remainder of this section
the shorthand pieces of notation

\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(0,H)}\qquad\text{ as well as }\qquad D=D_{X,W}\,.

We let
\mathbf{p}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathsf{BP}
and
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbf{p}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu:[\bar{T}_{H},T_{H}]\to\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathsf{Qd}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
be the
canonical projections, which are continuous since
D
and
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
are
continuous functions. Note that, clearly, for every
n
, it holds that

D_{\infty}\leq\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{n}
on
[\bar{\tau}_{h_{n}+1}+1,\tau_{h_{n}}]^{2}
, so
that
D\leq\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
on
[\bar{T}_{H},T_{H}]
. As a result, there exists a unique continuous (even
1
-Lipschitz) projection

\pi:\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathsf{Qd}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\to\mathbf{p}([\bar{T}_{H},T_{H}])
such that
\mathbf{p}=\pi\circ\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbf{p}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu

on
[\bar{T}_{H},T_{H}]
.

The inequality
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\leq\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
follows from the usual
following arguments. First we come back to discrete maps and observe
that, for integers
i
,
j\in[\bar{\tau}_{h_{n}+1}+1,\tau_{h_{n}}]
, we have
d_{C}(i,j)=0
if and only if
v_{i}

and
v_{j}
are the same vertex of
F_{n}\cup\bar{F}_{n}
, which implies that

\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{n}(i,j)=0
. Next, by considering the so-called
maximal
wedge path
consisting of the concatenation of the two geodesics
from
c_{i}
and from
c_{j}
obtained by following subsequent successors
up to the point where they coalesce, we obtain the classical upper
bound similar to (
5
):

\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{n}(i,j)\leq d_{\Lambda}(i,j)+2\,,\qquad i,j\in[\bar{\tau}_{h_{n}+1}+1,\tau_{h_{n}}]\ \text{ with }\ ij\geq 0\,.

(52)

Passing to the limit yields that
\{d_{X}=0\}\subseteq\{\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=0\}
and that
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\leq\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mud\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{W}
, which imply the inequality
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\leq\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
. The converse inequality is harder and is the focus of what follows.

Let us start with some key properties of the pseudometrics
D
,
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
,
and
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
. The following lemma is proved in the exact same way as
[
BM17
, Lemma 14]
.

Lemma 52
.

The spaces
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathsf{Qd}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
and
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}
are compact geodesic metric spaces.

We will need the
following identification of the set
\{\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=0\}
, analog to Lemma
46
.

Lemma 53
.

The following holds almost surely. For every
s
,
t\in[\bar{T}_{H},T_{H}]
with
s\neq t
, it
holds that
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)=0
if and only if either
d_{X}(s,t)=0
or

\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mud\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{W}(s,t)=0
, these two cases being mutually
exclusive.

Proof.

It follows very similar lines to that of
Proposition 3.1 in
[
LG13
]
, and we will only sketch the main
arguments. The fact that
d_{X}(s,t)=0
or
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mud\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{W}(s,t)=0
implies

\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)=0
is immediate from the inequality
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\leq\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
. Conversely, assume that
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)=0
for some
s\neq t

in
[\bar{T}_{H},T_{H}]
. Then, in particular,
since
D\leq\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, it holds that
D(s,t)=0
, so that either

d_{W}(s,t)=0
or
d_{X}(s,t)=0
, and these two cases are exclusive. If
we are in the case that
s
,
t
are of the same sign and that
d_{W}(s,t)=0
, this trivially
implies
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mud\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{W}(s,t)=0
, as wanted. And since
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mud\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{W}\geq d_{W}
, it cannot hold that
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mud\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{W}(s,t)=d_{X}(s,t)=0
at the same time. Hence, the proof will be complete if we can show that the situation where
d_{W}(s,t)=0
necessarily implies that
s
and
t
are of the same
sign.

For this, we argue by contradiction, assuming that
t<0<s
and

d_{W}(s,t)=0
.
Note that this implies in particular that
s
,
t
lie on some point of
the geodesics
\Gamma_{0}
and
\Xi_{0}
, respectively, meaning that

W_{s}=\inf_{u\in[0,s]}W_{u}
and
W_{t}=\inf_{u\in[t,0]}W_{u}
.
Then, by the convergence of
\Lambda_{(n)}
to
W
,
there exist
i_{n}\in[0,\tau_{h_{n}}]
and
j_{n}\in[\bar{\tau}_{h_{n}+1}+1,0]
such that
i_{n}/2n\to s
and
j_{n}/2n\to t
, with
the property that
\Lambda_{n}(i_{n})=\min_{k\in[0,i_{n}]}\Lambda_{n}(k)

and
\Lambda_{n}(j_{n})=\min_{k\in[j_{n},0]}\Lambda_{n}(k)
. This means
that
v_{i_{n}}
lies on the maximal geodesic
\gamma_{n}
of
Q_{n}
, and
v_{j_{n}}
lies at distance
1
from the shuttle
\bar{\xi}_{n}
of
Q_{n}
.

Now any geodesic path in
Q_{n}
from
v_{i_{n}}
to
v_{j_{n}}
will necessarily
intersect the spine of the tree
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
at some tree root

\rho^{l_{n}}
with
0\leq l_{n}\leq h_{n}
.
Let
k_{n}\in[\bar{\tau}_{h_{n}+1}+1,\tau_{h_{n}}]
be an integer such
that
v_{k_{n}}=\rho^{l_{n}}
. In terms of the contour process
C_{n}
,
this means that
C_{n}(k_{n})\leq C_{n}(l)
for every
l\in[0\wedge k_{n},0\vee k_{n}]
. Up to extracting along a further subsequence, we
may assume that
k_{n}/2n\to u\in[\bar{T}_{H},T_{H}]
as
n\to\infty
, and
we observe that
u
must be such that
X_{u}\leq X_{t}
for every
t\in[0\wedge u,0\vee u]
, and in particular, we observe that

d_{X}(u,T_{H^{\prime}})=d_{X}(u,\bar{T}_{H^{\prime}})=0
where
H^{\prime}=-X_{u}
. We may exclude
the case where
H^{\prime}=0
by noting that, necessarily,
W_{s}=W_{t}=W_{u}<0
.

On the other hand, since
v_{k_{n}}
lies on a
geodesic path from
v_{i_{n}}
to
v_{j_{n}}
, which has length

\scalebox{.5}[.62]{$\mathbfcal{O}$}(n^{1/4})
because of our assumption that
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)=0
, it
holds that
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,u)=\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(u,t)=0
. We arrive at the wanted
contradiction since we have found four points
s\neq t
,
T_{H^{\prime}}\neq\bar{T}_{H^{\prime}}
that are all identified by
D
but such that
d_{W}(s,t)=0
and

d_{X}(T_{H^{\prime}},\bar{T}_{H^{\prime}})=0
.
∎

As
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\leq\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\leq\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mud\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{W}
and
\{d_{X}=0\}\subseteq\{\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=0\}
, Lemma
53
implies that the
equivalence relations
\{\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=0\}
and
\{\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=0\}
coincide, and that
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbf{p}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbf{p}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(0,H)}
. For this reason we may, and will, systematically
identify points of
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathsf{Qd}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
with points of
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}
.
Moreover, the identity mapping
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}\to\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathsf{Qd}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
is
continuous, and by compactness of these spaces,
we conclude that
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathsf{Qd}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
is homeomorphic to
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}
.

Theorem
50
will be obtained by compactness and continuity arguments from the following local version, stating that, locally and away either from both maximal geodesics or from both shuttles, the three distances under consideration are equal. The proof of the following lemma can straightforwardly be adapted from
[
BM17
, Lemma 15]
, so that we only sketch it and refer the reader to the latter reference for the details. In an arbitrary pseudometric space
(M,d)
, we denote by
d(x,A)=\inf\{d(x,y):y\in A\}
the distance from a point
x\in M
to a subset
A\subseteq M
.

Lemma 54
.

The following holds almost surely. Fix
\varepsilon>0
, and let
s
,
t\in[\bar{T}_{H},T_{H}]
be such that
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)<\varepsilon
and

•

either
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\big(s,\Gamma_{0}\cup\Gamma_{\bar{T}_{H}}\big)\wedge\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\big(t,\Gamma_{0}\cup\Gamma_{\bar{T}_{H}}\big)>\varepsilon
;

•

or
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\big(s,\Xi_{0}\cup\Xi_{T_{H}}\big)\wedge\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\big(t,\Xi_{0}\cup\Xi_{T_{H}}\big)>\varepsilon
.

Then, it holds that
D(s,t)=\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)=\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)
.

Proof.

Let
i_{n}
,
j_{n}
be integers in
[\bar{\tau}_{h_{n}+1}+1,\tau_{h_{n}}]
such that

i_{n}/2n\to s
and
j_{n}/2n\to t
as
n\to\infty
. From the assumption
that
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)<\varepsilon
and the convergence of
D_{(n)}
toward

\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, we deduce that
d_{Q_{n}}(v_{i_{n}},v_{j_{n}})<\varepsilon(8n/9)^{1/4}

for every
n
large enough.

Next, keeping the same notation, assume that we are in the first
alternative of the statement. Then we claim that
for every
n
large enough,
v_{i_{n}}
and
v_{j_{n}}
must be at

d_{Q_{n}}
-distance at least
\varepsilon(8n/9)^{1/4}
from the maximal geodesics

\gamma_{n}
and
\bar{\gamma}_{n}
of
Q_{n}
. Indeed, if we assume otherwise,
then up to taking an extraction along a further subsequence, we would
find a point
k_{n}\in[\bar{\tau}_{h_{n}+1}+1,\tau_{h_{n}}]
such that for every

n
,
v_{k_{n}}

belongs to (the same) one of these maximal geodesics, and is at
d_{Q_{n}}
-distance at most

(8n/9)^{1/4}
from (the same) one of two points
v_{i_{n}}
or
v_{j_{n}}
. To fix the ideas,
assume that
v_{k_{n}}
is on
\gamma_{n}
and is close to
v_{i_{n}}
in
the latter sense, the discussion being similar in the other cases.
Up to taking yet another subsequence if necessary, we may assume that

k_{n}/2n
converges to some
u\in[\bar{T}_{H},T_{H}]
. Note that
k_{n}
,
being a time of visit of the maximal geodesic
\gamma_{n}
, must be a
left-minimum for the label process
\Lambda_{n}
restricted to nonnegative times, and, by passing to the limit,

u
must be a left-minimum of
W
restricted to nonnegative times, entailing
that
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(u,\Gamma_{0})=0
. Therefore, by passing to the limit in the
inequality
D_{(n)}(i_{n}/2n,k_{n}/2n)\leq\varepsilon
, we would obtain that

\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,\Gamma_{0})\leq\varepsilon
, a contradiction with our assumption.

Now observe that
Q_{\infty}
is obtained by the following two gluing operations, from
Q_{n}
and the infinite quadrangulation
Q_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}
, encoded by the labeled double forest with trees grafted above
\rho^{h_{n}+i}
,
i\geq 0
, and below
\rho^{h_{n}+i}
,
i\geq 1
.

•

First, by gluing the geodesic sides
\xi_{n}
and
\bar{\gamma}_{n}
of
Q_{n}
to the (unique) maximal geodesic and shuttle of
Q_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}
. Note that the resulting infinite quadrangulation is also obtained by performing the interval CVS construction on
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
with the intervals
\{c_{i},\,i\leq 0\}
and
\{c_{i},\,i\geq 0\}
.

•

Second, by gluing together the (unique) maximal geodesic and shuttle of the infinite quadrangulation obtained at the first step. Note that the geodesic sides of this infinite map are prolongations of
\gamma_{n}
and
\bar{\xi}_{n}
.

Therefore, Lemma
21
.
(ii)
applied twice (once for each gluing
operation) shows that if
v
,
w\in V(Q_{n})
are such that

d_{Q_{n}}(v,w)<K
and either

•

d_{Q_{n}}(v,\gamma_{n})\wedge d_{Q_{n}}(w,\gamma_{n})>K
,

•

or
d_{Q_{n}}(v,\bar{\gamma}_{n})\wedge d_{Q_{n}}(w,\bar{\gamma}_{n})>K
,

then
d_{Q_{n}}(v,w)=d_{Q_{\infty}}(v,w)
. Applying this to
v=v_{i_{n}}
,
w=v_{j_{n}}
,
and
K=(8n/9)^{1/4}\varepsilon
yields, after passing to the limit, that

\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)=D(s,t)
. Since
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\leq\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\leq D
, this yields the
result in the first alternative of the statement. The second case,
with shuttles instead of maximal geodesics, is similar.
∎

We may finally prove Theorem
50
.

Proof of Theorem
50
.

We follow the same lines as in the proof of
[
BM17
, Theorem 11]
. As we observed before, the metric spaces
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathsf{Qd}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu

and
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}
are homeomorphic. Therefore, since the geodesics
\gamma
and
\bar{\gamma}
do not intersect in
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}
, the same
is true in
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathsf{Qd}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, and similarly, the geodesics
\xi
and
\bar{\xi}

do not intersect in these spaces. Moreover, as we know, these four
geodesics intersect only at
\gamma(0)=\bar{\xi}(0)
,
\xi(0)=\bar{\gamma}(0)
,
x_{\smash{*}}^{(0,H)}
and
\bar{x}_{\smash{*}}^{(0,H)}
. Therefore, for every

x\in\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathsf{Qd}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\setminus\big\{\gamma(0),\bar{\gamma}(0),x_{\smash{*}}^{(0,H)},\bar{x}_{\smash{*}}^{(0,H)}\big\}
, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that the open ball

B_{\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}(x,\varepsilon)
of
radius
\varepsilon
around
x
for the metric
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
intersects neither
\gamma\cup\bar{\gamma}
nor
\xi\cup\bar{\xi}
. By Lemma
54
, this implies that the balls
B_{\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}(x,\varepsilon)
,
and
B_{\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}(x,\varepsilon)
are isometric. Hence,
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathsf{Qd}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
and

\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}
are two compact geodesic metric spaces that are locally
isometric except possibly around four points. Therefore, the lengths
of paths that do not go through these four points must be the same in
both spaces. It is then easy to see that the same is true for all
paths that visit each of these four points at most once, by splitting into
subpaths, and by standard properties of lengths of paths.
One concludes by observing that, given a path in
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathsf{Qd}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, one may
construct another path of length smaller than or equal to that of the
initial path, and that visits each of the four distinguished points at
most once. Since a geodesic space is a length space
[
BBI01
]
,
the distance between two points is given by the infimum of length of
paths between these points. Therefore,
\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0mu\mathsf{Qd}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
and
\mathsf{Qd}^{(0,H)}

are isometric.
∎

6.6
Scaling limit of conditioned quadrilaterals

In this section, we finally prove Theorem
14
. As a preliminary result, we will need a simple estimate on distances
in quadrilaterals. We invite the reader to recall the combinatorial setting of
Section
2.4
and to consult Figure
17
. Let
((\mathbf{f},\bar{\mathbf{f}}),\lambda)
be a well-labeled double forest and let
\mathbf{q}
be the corresponding quadrilateral. For
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,h-1\}
, keeping only the
first

k
trees in
\mathbf{f}
and
the
last

k
trees in
\bar{\mathbf{f}}
yields a submap of
\mathbf{f}\cup\bar{\mathbf{f}}
, well labeled by the restriction of
\lambda
. We let
\mathbf{q}_{k}
be the corresponding quadrilateral, which we naturally see as a submap of
\mathbf{q}
. We will need the following coarse comparison between distances in
\mathbf{q}
and
\mathbf{q}_{k}
.

Figure 17:
Here,
h=5
and
k=3
. On the left, a schematic picture of a double forest
(\mathbf{f},\bar{\mathbf{f}})
, assumed to be well labeled, and its “truncation” obtained after removing the dashed elements. On the right, a schematic picture of the corresponding quadrilaterals: the quadrilateral
\mathbf{q}
is obtained by gluing
\mathbf{q}_{k}
(in light yellow) along its sides
\xi
and
\bar{\gamma}
with the quadrilateral (in orange) coded by the dashed elements along its sides
\gamma
and
\bar{\xi}
(only these four geodesic sides of interest are named in the picture).

Lemma 55
.

Let
\varpi=2+\max\{\lambda(u):u\in V(\mathbf{q})\setminus V(\mathbf{q}_{k})\}-\min\{\lambda(u):u\in V(\mathbf{q})\setminus V(\mathbf{q}_{k})\}
. Then, for any
v
,
w\in V(\mathbf{q}_{k})
, one has

d_{\mathbf{q}}(v,w)\leq d_{\mathbf{q}_{k}}(v,w)\leq d_{\mathbf{q}}(v,w)+\varpi\,.

Proof.

Observe that
\mathbf{q}
may be obtained by
gluing
\mathbf{q}_{k}
along its sides
\xi
and
\bar{\gamma}
with the quadrilateral coded by the double forest obtained by taking the last
h-k
trees in
\mathbf{f}
and
the first
h-k
trees in
\bar{\mathbf{f}}
, well labeled by the restriction of
\lambda
, along its sides
\gamma
and
\bar{\xi}
. The lemma is then a straightforward consequence of Lemma
21
.
(i)
since the lengths of the glued geodesics are bounded by the quantity
\varpi
.
∎

We now prove Theorem
14
by proceeding similarly as in Section
5.5
. Recall the
notation
(C(t),\Lambda(t),t\in\mathbb{R})
,
\tau_{k}
,
\bar{\tau}_{k}
from Section
6.4
. Let
\mathbb{P}_{\infty}
be the law of
(C,\Lambda)
and assume
without loss of generality that the latter is the canonical
process. Although we use the same notation
\mathbb{P}_{\infty}
as
in Section
5.5
, we believe that there is
little risk of confusion. For
j\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{F}_{j}
be the

\sigma
-algebra generated by
(C(i),\Lambda(i),0\leq i\leq j)
, and
let
\mathcal{G}_{j}
be the one generated by
(C(i),\Lambda(i+1),-j\leq i\leq-1)
. Note that

\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{h}}
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the
h
leftmost
trees of
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
in the upper half-plane, together
with their labels, as well as the label of the root
\rho^{h}
. Similarly,

\mathcal{G}_{-\bar{\tau}_{h+1}}
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the
h

leftmost trees of
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
in the lower half-plane, together with their
labels, as well as the label of the root
\rho^{0}
: the meaning of the shift by
+1
in the process
\Lambda
is
that we do not want to incorporate the information of the label of the
root
\rho^{h+1}
in
\mathcal{G}_{-\bar{\tau}_{h+1}}
.

Next, for
a
,
\bar{a}
,
h\in\mathbb{N}
and

\delta\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote by
\mathbb{P}_{a,\bar{a},h,\delta}
the distribution of

\big(C{\raisebox{-1.49994pt}{$|$}}{}_{[-2\bar{a}-h,2a+h]},\Lambda{\raisebox{-1.49994pt}{$|$}}{}_{[-2\bar{a}-h,2a+h]}\big)

under
\mathbb{P}_{\infty}[\;\cdot\mid\tau_{h}=2a+h,\,\bar{\tau}_{h+1}+1=-2\bar{a}-h,\,\Lambda(\tau_{h})=\delta]
. The
corresponding double forest encoded by this random process is thus composed
of a spine
\rho^{0}
, …,
\rho^{h}
of length
h
, on which are grafted
2h
Bienaymé–Galton–Watson trees with Geometric(
1/2
)
offspring distribution and uniform admissible labels, conditioned on
the fact that the total number of edges in the upper half-plane
h
trees is
a
,
the number of edges in the lower half-plane
h
trees is
\bar{a}
, and the
label of the last root
\rho^{h}
is
\delta
. The following lemma gives an absolute continuity relation between the
laws
\mathbb{P}_{a,\bar{a},h,\delta}
and
\mathbb{P}_{\infty}
. Its proof, which we
omit, is similar to that of Lemma
36
, using the enumeration results of Proposition
11
. Recall from Propositions
9
and
11
the definitions of
Q_{\ell}
and
M_{\ell}
.

Lemma 56
.

Fix the integers
0<k<h
, as well as positive integers
a
,
\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}

and
\delta\in\mathbb{Z}
.
For every nonnegative functional
G
that is
\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{k}}\vee\mathcal{G}_{-\bar{\tau}_{k+1}}
-measurable, we
have

\mathbb{E}_{a,\bar{a},h,\delta}[G]=\mathbb{E}_{\infty}\big[\Psi_{a,\bar{a},h,\delta}(\tau_{k},-(\bar{\tau}_{k+1}+1),k,\Lambda(\tau_{k}))\cdot G\big]\,,

where

\Psi_{a,\bar{a},h,\delta}(s,t,h^{\prime},j)=\frac{Q_{h-h^{\prime}}(2a+h-s)}{Q_{h}(2a+h)}\frac{Q_{h-h^{\prime}}(2\bar{a}+h-t)}{Q_{h}(2\bar{a}+h)}\frac{M_{h-h^{\prime}}(\delta-j)}{M_{h}(\delta)}\,.

From now on, in addition to the sequence
(h_{n})
, we fix three sequences
(a_{n})
,
(\bar{a}_{n})
,
(\delta_{n})
as in (
9
). The following is a tedious but
straightforward consequence of the local limit theorem
[
BGT89
, Theorem 8.4.1]
.

Lemma 57
.

If the integer-valued sequence
(h^{\prime}_{n})
satisfies

h^{\prime}_{n}/\sqrt{2n}\to H^{\prime}\in(0,H)
, then

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s\leq a_{n}\\[2.0pt]
0\leq t\leq\bar{a}_{n}\end{subarray},\,j\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|\Psi_{a_{n},\bar{a}_{n},h_{n},\delta_{n}}(s,t,h^{\prime}_{n},j)-\psi_{A,\bar{A},H^{\prime},\Delta}\Big(\frac{s}{n},\frac{t}{n},H^{\prime},\Big(\frac{9}{8n}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}j\Big)\right|\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits_{n\to\infty}}0\,.

We proceed to the conditioned version of Theorem
50
. Recall the definition of
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{(n)}
given in (
50
).

Proposition 58
.

On
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}^{(2)}
, the triple
\big(C_{(n)},\Lambda_{(n)},\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{(n)}\big)
considered under
\mathbb{P}_{a_{n},\bar{a}_{n},h_{n},\delta_{n}}
converges in distribution to
\big(X,W,\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{X,W}=\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(0,H)}\big)
, considered under
\mathbf{Quad}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}
.

Proof.

The arguments are very close to those used in the proof of Proposition
38
in Section
5.5
, adding Lemma
55
and Proposition
40
to cover the additional difficulty. The joint convergence of the first two coordinates is also standard. Then, fix
\varepsilon\in(0,H)
and set
h^{\varepsilon}_{n}=h_{n}-\lfloor\varepsilon\sqrt{2n}\rfloor
. Let
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\varepsilon}_{n}
and
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\varepsilon}_{(n)}
be defined as in (
50
) and above, but with
h_{n}^{\varepsilon}
instead of
h_{n}
. For simplicity, for every
i\in\mathbb{R}
, let

i^{\varepsilon}=(\bar{\tau}_{h^{\varepsilon}_{n}+1}+1)\vee i\wedge\tau_{h^{\varepsilon}_{n}}

and define
j^{\varepsilon}
similarly for any
j\in\mathbb{R}
. Define also

\kappa^{\varepsilon}_{n}=(\tau_{h_{n}}-\tau_{h^{\varepsilon}_{n}})+(\bar{\tau}_{h^{\varepsilon}_{n}+1}-\bar{\tau}_{h_{n}+1})
. From (
52
), we obtain

\displaystyle\big|\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{n}(i,j)-\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{n}(i^{\varepsilon},j^{\varepsilon})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{n}(i,i^{\varepsilon})+\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{n}(j,j^{\varepsilon})\leq 4\big(\omega(\Lambda_{n};\kappa^{\varepsilon}_{n})+1\big)\,.

Using Lemma
55
, we have for every
i
,
j\in[\bar{\tau}_{h^{\varepsilon}_{n}+1}+1,\tau_{h^{\varepsilon}_{n}}]
,

\big|\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{n}(i,j)-\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{n}^{\varepsilon}(i,j)\big|\leq\omega(\Lambda_{n};\kappa^{\varepsilon}_{n})+2\,.

These two facts together then imply that

\mathrm{dist}_{\mathcal{C}^{(2)}}\big(\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\varepsilon}_{(n)},\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{(n)}\big)\leq\frac{\kappa^{\varepsilon}_{n}}{2n}+5\omega\big(\Lambda_{(n)},\kappa^{\varepsilon}_{n}/2n\big)+\mathcal{O}\big(n^{-1/4}\big)\,.

We now use the convergence of the first two coordinates, implying, for every
\eta>0
,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{a_{n},\bar{a}_{n},h_{n},\delta_{n}}\left(\mathrm{dist}_{\mathcal{C}^{(2)}}\big(\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\varepsilon}_{(n)},\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{(n)}\big)\geq\eta\right)\leq\mathbf{Quad}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}\big(\kappa^{\varepsilon}+5\omega(W;\kappa^{\varepsilon})\geq\eta\big),

(53)

where
\kappa^{\varepsilon}=A-T_{H-\varepsilon}+\bar{T}_{H-\varepsilon}+\bar{A}
. Since a.s. under

\mathbf{Quad}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}
, the quantity
\kappa^{\varepsilon}
tends to
0
as

\varepsilon\to 0
, we
deduce that the left-hand side in (
53
) also converges to
0
. It
remains to show that
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\varepsilon}_{(n)}
under

\mathbb{P}_{a_{n},\bar{a}_{n},h_{n},\delta_{n}}
converges to

\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(0,H-\varepsilon)}
under
\mathbf{Quad}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}
to
conclude, by the principle of accompanying laws, that

\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{(n)}
converges to the distributional limit of

\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(0,H-\varepsilon)}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, which is

\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(0,H)}
by Proposition
40
.
To this end, we consider the restrictions

C^{\varepsilon}_{(n)}
,
\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{(n)}
of
C_{(n)}
,
\Lambda_{(n)}
to the
intervals
[(\bar{\tau}_{h^{\varepsilon}_{n}+1}+1)/2n,\tau_{h^{\varepsilon}_{n}}/2n]
and, letting
F
be a
nonnegative bounded continuous function, we observe that, using Lemma
56
, then Theorem
50
(for the choice of
H-\varepsilon
instead of
H
) and Lemma
57
, and finally Lemma
47
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{a_{n},\bar{a}_{n},h_{n},\delta_{n}}\big[F\big(C^{\varepsilon}_{(n)},\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{(n)},D^{\varepsilon}_{(n)}\big)\big]\\
=\mathbb{E}_{\infty}\big[\Psi_{a_{n},\bar{a}_{n},h_{n},\delta_{n}}(\tau_{h^{\varepsilon}_{n}},-1-\bar{\tau}_{h^{\varepsilon}_{n}+1},\Lambda(\tau_{h^{\varepsilon}_{n}}))\,G\big(C^{\varepsilon}_{(n)},\Lambda^{\varepsilon}_{(n)},D^{\varepsilon}_{(n)}\big)\big]\\
\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits_{n\to\infty}}\mathbf{Plane}\left[\psi_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}(T_{H-\varepsilon},-\bar{T}_{H-\varepsilon},H-\varepsilon,W_{T_{H-\varepsilon}})\,G\big(X^{(0,H-\varepsilon)},W^{(0,H-\varepsilon)},\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(0,H-\varepsilon)}\big)\right]\\
=\mathbf{Quad}_{A,\bar{A},H,\Delta}\big[G\big(X^{(0,H-\varepsilon)},W^{(0,H-\varepsilon)},\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(0,H-\varepsilon)}\big)\big]\,.

This concludes the proof.
∎

From there, we easily obtain the wanted GHP convergence by arguments similar as those developed in the proof of Theorem
12
at the end of Section
5.5
.

7
Construction from a continuous unicellular map

Our proof of Theorem
1
gives a description of the limiting
Brownian surfaces as gluings of elementary pieces, which appear either
in the Brownian plane or in the Brownian half-plane. Although this construction
has a clear geometric content, it can be arguably cumbersome to work
with, having in mind, for instance, the universal character that the
spaces
\mathsf{S}^{[g]}_{{\boldsymbol{L}}}
are expected to bear.

Indeed, we believe that Brownian surfaces arise as universal limits
for many more classes of maps satisfying mild conditions (for instance
uniformly distributed maps) and a more direct description seems to be useful in order to show
such results. In particular, we believe that the Brownian torus is the
scaling limit of essentially
simple triangulations, as considered in
[
BHL19
]
. In fact, most of the known results of
convergence toward the Brownian sphere use a re-rooting technique due to
Le Gall
[
LG13
]
, which, very roughly speaking, says that, if
maps in a given class are properly encoded by discrete objects
converging to the random snake driven by a normalized Brownian excursion and
if these maps and the limiting object exhibit a property of invariance
under uniform re-rooting, then the limiting space is the Brownian
sphere. We expect this approach to be generalizable to our context and
we now give a description of Brownian surfaces that is a direct
generalization of the classical definition of the Brownian
sphere. This can be thought of a continuum version of the
Cori–Vauquelin–Schaeffer bijection, building on a continuum version
of a unicellular map (a map with only one internal face).

For a function
f\in\mathcal{C}
and
s
,
t\in I(f)
with
t<s
, we extend (
21
) by setting

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muf\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)=\inf_{I(f)\setminus[t,s]}f

and we set, for
s
,
t\in I(f)
,

\tilde{d}_{f}(s,t)=f(s)+f(t)-2\max\big\{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muf\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t),\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muf\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(t,s)\big\}\,.

(54)

The difference with (
22
) is that we now take into account the
minimum of
f
on the “interval” from
s\vee t
to
s\wedge t
on
the “circle”
I(f)/\{\bar{\tau}(f)=\tau(f)\}
.

The Brownian sphere.

As a warm-up, let us first recall the definition of the Brownian
sphere. It is the metric space
\mathsf{S}^{[0]}_{\varnothing}=\big([0,1],\tilde{d}_{Z}\big)/\{d_{\mathbf{e}}=0\}
, where
Z
is the random snake driven by a normalized Brownian excursion
\mathbf{e}
.

Recall that the Continuum Random Tree (CRT) introduced by Aldous
[
Ald91
,
Ald93
]
is the
\mathbb{R}
-tree
15
15

15

See Section
5.1.1
.

\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{e}}=([0,1]/\{d_{\mathbf{e}}=0\},d_{\mathbf{e}})
, so that the Brownian sphere
\mathsf{S}^{[0]}_{\varnothing}
may actually be seen as a quotient of the CRT. In fact, Le Gall
[
LG07
]
showed that the pseudometric
\tilde{d}_{Z}/\{d_{\mathbf{e}}=0\}(s,t)=0
if and only if
\tilde{d}_{Z}(s,t)=0
or
d_{\mathbf{e}}(s,t)=0
, so that the topological space
\mathsf{S}^{[0]}_{\varnothing}
is obtained by a continuous analog to the Cori–Vauquelin–Schaeffer bijection.

The Brownian disk.

Let us turn to the Brownian disk with perimeter
L\in(0,\infty)
. It is the metric space
\mathsf{S}^{[0]}_{(L)}=\big([0,1],\tilde{d}_{W}\big)/\{d_{X}=0\}
, where
(X,W)
is the pair encoding a slice with area
1
, width
L
and tilt
0
, that is, distributed according to
\mathbf{Slice}_{1,L,0}
(defined in Section
5.2
).

The most natural continuous object generalizing the CRT in the case of the disk is the gluing
\mathscr{M}^{[0]}_{(L)}=([0,1],d_{X})/R
where
R
is the coarsest equivalence relation containing
\{d_{X}=0\}
and
\{(0,1)\}
. As
\tilde{d}_{W}(0,1)=0
, the Brownian disk is also
([0,1],\tilde{d}_{W})/R
and can be seen as a quotient of
\mathscr{M}^{[0]}_{(L)}
. Visually,
\mathscr{M}^{[0]}_{(L)}
is obtained by taking a circle of length
L
and gluing a Brownian forest of mass
1
and length
L
on it. The random snake
W
then assigns Brownian labels to it (with a Brownian bridge multiplied by
\sqrt{3}
on the circle and standard Brownian motions everywhere else).

The general case.

The CRT and the structure
\mathscr{M}^{[0]}_{(L)}
are the continuous
equivalent to the encoding objects of Section
2.2
in the
particular cases of the sphere and the disk. In general, we have a
similar yet even more intricate construction, which we now
describe. Let
g\geq 0
be fixed and
{\boldsymbol{L}}=(L^{1},\dots,L^{b})
be a

b
-tuple of positive real numbers. Let then

\big(S,\left({A^{e}}\right)_{e\in\vec{E}(S)},\left({H^{e}}\right)_{e\in\vec{I}(S)},\left(L^{e}\right)_{e\in\vec{B}(S)},\left(\Lambda^{v}\right)_{v\in V(S)}\big)
be a random vector distributed according to the distribution
\mathrm{Param}_{\boldsymbol{L}}
, defined around (
18
). Conditionally
given this vector, we consider the following collection of
processes. For each
e\in\vec{E}(S)
,

•

the process
X^{e}
is a first-passage bridge of standard Brownian motion from
0
to
-H^{e}
with duration
A^{e}
;

•

the process
Z^{e}
is a random snake driven by the reflected process
X^{e}-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{e}
;

the processes
(X^{e},Z^{e})
,
e\in\vec{E}(S)
, being independent. Independently, the process
\zeta^{e}
is a Brownian bridge

•

of duration
H^{e}
from
\Lambda^{e^{-}}
to
\Lambda^{e^{+}}
, with variance
1
if
e\in\vec{I}(S)
;

•

of duration
L^{e}
from
\Lambda^{e^{-}}
to
\Lambda^{e^{+}}
, with variance
3
if
e\in\vec{B}(S)
.

Furthermore, for
e\in\vec{I}(S)
, the bridges are linked through the relation

\zeta^{\bar{e}}(s)=\zeta^{e}(H^{e}-s)\,,\qquad 0\leq s\leq H^{e}\,,

and, except for these relations, are independent. We then set, for each
e\in\vec{E}(S)
,

W^{e}_{t}=Z^{e}_{t}+\zeta^{e}_{-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{e}_{t}}\,,\qquad 0\leq t\leq A^{e}\,.

In the end, we obtain a collection of processes
(X^{e},W^{e})
,
e\in\vec{E}(S)
, which are linked through the relations linking
\zeta^{e}
with
\zeta^{\bar{e}}
, translating the fact that the labels of the floors of forests grafted on both sides of the same internal edge of the scheme should correspond.

We arrange the half-edges
e_{1}
, …,
e_{\kappa}
incident to the internal face of
S
according to the contour order, starting from the root, and we define the concatenation

(W_{s})_{0\leq s\leq 1}=W^{e_{1}}\bullet\dots\bullet W^{e_{\kappa}}\,,

which is a continuous process. We define
\tilde{d}_{W}
by (
54
) as above and now define the equivalence relation along which to glue.

Roughly speaking, we glue together Brownian forests coded by the
X^{e}
’s according to the scheme structure. For
s\in[0,1)
, we denote by
[s]
the integer in
\{1,\dots,\kappa\}
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{[s]-1}A^{e_{i}}\leq s<\sum_{i=1}^{[s]}A^{e_{i}}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\langle s\rangle=s-\sum_{i=1}^{[s]-1}A^{e_{i}}\in\big[0,A^{e_{[s]}}\big)\,.

By convention, we also set
[1]=1
and
\langle 1\rangle=0
. We define the relation
R
on
[0,1]
as the coarsest equivalence relation for which
s\mathbin{R}t
if one of the following occurs:

\displaystyle[s]=[t]\quad\text{ and }\quad d_{X^{e_{[s]}}}(\langle s\rangle,\langle t\rangle)=0\,;

(55a)

\displaystyle e_{[s]}=\bar{e}_{[t]},\ X^{{[s]}}\langle s\rangle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{{[s]}}\langle s\rangle,\ X^{{[t]}}\langle t\rangle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{{[t]}}\langle t\rangle\ \text{ and }\ X^{{[s]}}\langle s\rangle=H^{e_{[t]}}-X^{{[t]}}\langle t\rangle\,;

(55b)

where we wrote
X^{[s]}\langle s\rangle
instead of
X^{e_{[s]}}(\langle s\rangle)
for short. Equation (
55a
) identifies numbers coding the same point in one of the Brownian forests, while Equation (
55b
) identifies the floors of forests “facing each other”: the numbers
s
and
t
should code floor points (second and third equalities) of forests facing each other (first equality) and correspond to the same point (fourth equality).

Proposition 59
.

The Brownian surface
\mathsf{S}^{[g]}_{{\boldsymbol{L}}}
has the same distribution as
([0,1],\tilde{d}_{W})/R
.

Let us give a similar interpretation as in the case of the disk. Let first
(X_{s})_{0\leq s\leq 1}
be the continuous process obtained by shifting and concatenating
X^{e_{1}}
, …,
X^{e_{\kappa}}
. Then
\mathsf{S}^{[g]}_{{\boldsymbol{L}}}
may be seen as a quotient of
\mathscr{M}^{[g]}_{{\boldsymbol{L}}}=([0,1],d_{X})/R
, which can be pictured as follows. Starting from the random vector
\big(S,\left({A^{e}}\right)_{e\in\vec{E}(S)},\left({H^{e}}\right)_{e\in\vec{I}(S)},\left(L^{e}\right)_{e\in\vec{B}(S)}\big)
, we first construct the metric graph obtained from
S
by assigning either the length
H^{e}
or
L^{e}
to the edge corresponding to
e
. For every half-edge
e
incident to the internal face of
S
, we then glue a Brownian forest of mass
A^{e}
and length
H^{e}
or
L^{e}
on
e
. We equip this space
\mathscr{M}^{[g]}_{{\boldsymbol{L}}}
with Brownian labels (with variance
\sqrt{3}
on the boundary edges) and define
\mathsf{S}^{[g]}_{{\boldsymbol{L}}}
from there by the same process as in the case of the Brownian disk.

Proof of Proposition
59
.

First of all, recall from Section
4.2
that the Brownian surface
\mathsf{S}^{[g]}_{{\boldsymbol{L}}}
is defined as the gluing along geodesic sides of a collection of continuum elementary pieces distributed as follows. Conditionally given

\big(S,\left({A^{e}}\right)_{e\in\vec{E}(S)},\left({H^{e}}\right)_{e\in\vec{I}(S)},\left(L^{e}\right)_{e\in\vec{B}(S)},\left(\Lambda^{v}\right)_{v\in V(S)}\big)\,,

the elementary pieces
\mathsf{EP}^{e}
,
e\in\vec{E}(S)
, are only dependent through the relation linking
\mathsf{EP}^{e}
with
\mathsf{EP}^{\bar{e}}
and, setting
\Delta^{e}=\Lambda^{e^{+}}-\Lambda^{e^{-}}
,

•

if
e\in\vec{B}(S)
, then
\mathsf{EP}^{e}
is a slice with area
A^{e}
, width
L^{e}
and tilt
\Delta^{e}
;

•

if
e\in\vec{I}(S)
, then
\mathsf{EP}^{e}
is a quadrilateral with half-areas
A^{e}
and
A^{\bar{e}}
, width
H^{e}
and tilt
\Delta^{e}
.

Furthermore, it is straightforward from the definition of the pairs
(X^{e},W^{e})
,
e\in\vec{E}(S)
, that, if
e\in\vec{B}(S)
, then the pair
(X^{e},W^{e}-\Lambda^{e^{-}})
is distributed as
\mathbf{Slice}_{A^{e},L^{e},\Delta^{e}}
.
When
e\in\vec{I}(S)
, we denote by

\big(\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{e},\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0muW\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{e}\big)=\big(X^{e}_{s+A^{e}}-2\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{e}_{s+A^{e}}-H^{e},W^{e}_{s+A^{e}}\big)_{-A^{e}\leq s\leq 0}

the process obtained by shifting the Pitman transform of
X^{e}
in order to obtain a process from
-H^{e}
to
0
, as well as changing the time range to
[-A^{e},0]
. By standard results on Brownian motion and random snakes, the pair obtained by concatenating
(\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\bar{e}},\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0muW\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\bar{e}}-\Lambda^{\bar{e}^{-}})
with
(X^{e},W^{e}-\Lambda^{e^{-}})
has the law of a process distributed as
\mathbf{Quad}_{A^{e},A^{\bar{e}},H^{e},\Delta^{e}}
.

As a result, we may assume that the elementary piece
\mathsf{EP}^{e}
is encoded by

•

the pair
(X^{e},W^{e}-\Lambda^{e^{-}})
if
e\in\vec{B}(S)
;

•

the concatenation of
(\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\bar{e}},\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0muW\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\bar{e}}-\Lambda^{\bar{e}^{-}})
with
(X^{e},W^{e}-\Lambda^{e^{-}})
if
e\in\vec{I}(S)
.

This yields a collection of elementary pieces with the proper laws and dependencies; the fact that, for
e\in\vec{I}(S)
,
\mathsf{EP}^{e}
and
\mathsf{EP}^{\bar{e}}
are the same with exchanged shuttles and maximal geodesics is a simple application of the Pitman transform.

For
s\in[0,1]
, we denote by
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(s)
the projection in the gluing
\mathsf{S}^{[g]}_{{\boldsymbol{L}}}
of the point
\langle s\rangle
of the elementary piece
\mathsf{EP}^{e_{[s]}}
. We claim that
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}:[0,1]\to\mathsf{S}^{[g]}_{{\boldsymbol{L}}}
is onto. Indeed, for each half-edge
\epsilon\in\vec{E}(S)
, recall that the elementary piece
\mathsf{EP}^{\epsilon}
is defined as a quotient of
[0,A^{\epsilon}]
and observe that
\{\langle s\rangle\,:s\text{ such that }e_{[s]}=\epsilon\}=[0,A^{\epsilon})
; furthermore, the “missing point”
A^{\epsilon}
of
\mathsf{EP}^{\epsilon}
is glued to a point
0
of some elementary piece, which is
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(s)
for some
s
satisfying
\langle s\rangle=0
. Writing
\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{S}}
the distance in
\mathsf{S}^{[g]}_{{\boldsymbol{L}}}
and
\mathrm{d}_{R}=\tilde{d}_{W}/R
, it is sufficient to show that, for
s
,
t\in[0,1]
,

\mathrm{d}_{R}(s,t)=\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{S}}\big({\boldsymbol{\pi}}(s),{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(t)\big).

As the pseudometric
d_{f}
defined in (
22
) is unchanged by the addition of an additive constant, setting

\mathrm{d}_{\epsilon}=\begin{cases}d_{W^{\epsilon}}&\text{ if }\epsilon\in\vec{B}(S)\\
\mkern 1.0mu\widehat{\mkern-1.0mud\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0muW\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\bar{\epsilon}}\bullet W^{\epsilon}}&\text{ if }\epsilon\in\vec{I}(S)\end{cases},

the quantity
\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{S}}\big({\boldsymbol{\pi}}(s),{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(t)\big)
