is the infimum of sums of the form
\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{d}_{\epsilon_{i}}(s_{i},t_{i})
where

•

\epsilon_{1}=e_{[s]}
,
s_{1}=\langle s\rangle
,
\epsilon_{\ell}=e_{[t]}
,
s_{\ell}=\langle t\rangle
;

•

for all
i
, it holds that
s_{i}
,
t_{i}\in\begin{cases}[0,A^{\epsilon_{i}}]&\text{ if }\epsilon_{i}\in\vec{B}(S)\\
[-A^{\bar{\epsilon}_{i}},A^{\epsilon_{i}}]&\text{ if }\epsilon_{i}\in\vec{I}(S)\end{cases}
;

•

for all
i
,

a)

either
\epsilon_{i}=\epsilon_{i+1}\in\vec{B}(S)
and
d_{X^{\epsilon_{i}}}(t_{i},s_{i+1})=0
;

b)

or
\epsilon_{i}=\epsilon_{i+1}\in\vec{I}(S)
and
d_{\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\bar{\epsilon}_{i}}\bullet X^{\epsilon_{i}}}(t_{i},s_{i+1})=0
;

c)

or the point
t_{i}
of
\mathsf{EP}^{\epsilon_{i}}
is glued to the point
s_{i+1}
of
\mathsf{EP}^{\epsilon_{i+1}}
.

As
\mathrm{d}_{\epsilon}(u,v)=\infty
whenever
uv<0
, we may furthermore assume that, for all
i
,
s_{i}t_{i}\geq 0
. Now, for each
i
, we set

\tilde{s}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{[\epsilon_{i}]-1}A^{e_{j}}+s_{i}\ \text{ if }s_{i}\geq 0\,,\qquad\tilde{s}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{[\bar{\epsilon}_{i}]}A^{e_{j}}+s_{i}\ \text{ if }s_{i}<0\,,

where we wrote
[\epsilon]
the index of the half-edge
\epsilon
in the ordering
e_{1}
, …,
e_{\kappa}
of
\vec{E}(S)
. We define
\tilde{t}_{i}
similarly. It is easy to check that
\tilde{s}_{1}=s
,
\tilde{t}_{\ell}=t
and that, for each
i
, we have
\mathrm{d}_{\epsilon_{i}}(s_{i},t_{i})=d_{W}(\tilde{s}_{i},\tilde{t}_{i})
. Furthermore, for each
i
, we have the following.

a)

If
\epsilon_{i}=\epsilon_{i+1}\in\vec{B}(S)
and
d_{X^{\epsilon_{i}}}(t_{i},s_{i+1})=0
, then, unless
t_{i}=s_{i+1}=A^{\epsilon_{i}}
(in which case
\tilde{t}_{i}=\tilde{s}_{i+1}
), it holds that
s_{i}<A^{\epsilon_{i}}
and
t_{i}<A^{\epsilon_{i}}
, which yields that
\tilde{t}_{i}\mathbin{R}\tilde{s}_{i+1}
by (
55a
).

b)

If
\epsilon_{i}=\epsilon_{i+1}\in\vec{I}(S)
and
d_{\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\bar{\epsilon}_{i}}\bullet X^{\epsilon_{i}}}(t_{i},s_{i+1})=0
, then,

–

if
t_{i}s_{i+1}\geq 0
, then
\tilde{t}_{i}\mathbin{R}\tilde{s}_{i+1}
by (
55a
) as above;

–

if
t_{i}s_{i+1}<0
, then
\tilde{t}_{i}\mathbin{R}\tilde{s}_{i+1}
by (
55b
).

c)

If the point
t_{i}
of
\mathsf{EP}^{\epsilon_{i}}
is glued to the point
s_{i+1}
of
\mathsf{EP}^{\epsilon_{i+1}}
, then it implies that
\tilde{d}_{W}(\tilde{t}_{i},\tilde{s}_{i+1})=0
(recall the situation depicted in Figure
11
).

As a result, since
\tilde{d}_{W}\leq d_{W}
, it holds that
\mathrm{d}_{R}(s,t)\leq\mathrm{d}_{\mathsf{S}}\big({\boldsymbol{\pi}}(s),{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(t)\big)
. The converse inequality is very similar, noting that
R
identifies points in the elementary piece
\mathsf{EP}^{\epsilon}
as does
d_{X^{\epsilon}}=0
or
d_{\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\bar{\epsilon}}\bullet X^{\epsilon}}=0
, and that
\tilde{d}_{W}
encodes all the functions
\mathrm{d}_{\epsilon}
, together with the gluings of the elementary pieces. The use of
\tilde{d}_{W}
and not
d_{W}
takes into account the gluings of shuttles with maximal geodesics of elementary pieces “overflying” the root, as
\mathsf{EP}^{e_{14}}
with
\mathsf{EP}^{e_{1}}
or
\mathsf{EP}^{e_{12}}
with
\mathsf{EP}^{e_{7}}
in Figure
11
for instance. The details are left to the reader.
∎

Appendix A
Technical lemmas on the Brownian plane

We now recall the definition of the Brownian plane from
[
CLG14
]
, then show that it is equivalent to the one we gave in Section
6.3
, and we finally prove Proposition
49
.

A.1
Equivalence of definitions of the Brownian plane

The original definition goes as follows. Let
(\mathfrak{X}_{t},t\in\mathbb{R})
be such that
(\mathfrak{X}_{t},t\geq 0)
and

(\mathfrak{X}_{-t},t\geq 0)
are two independent three-dimensional Bessel processes. Since the overall minimum of
\mathfrak{X}
is reached at
0
, the maximum in the definition of
\tilde{d}_{\mathfrak{X}}
– given in (
54
) – is equal to

\max\big(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathfrak{X}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t),\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathfrak{X}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(t,s)\big)=\begin{cases}\inf\{\mathfrak{X}_{u},u\in[s\wedge t,s\vee t]\}&\text{ if }st\geq 0\\
\inf\{\mathfrak{X}_{u},u\notin[s\wedge t,s\vee t]\}&\text{ if }st<0\end{cases}\,.

Next, define
(\mathfrak{W}_{t},t\in\mathbb{R})
to be a centered Gaussian process
conditionally given
\mathfrak{X}
, with covariance function specified by

\mathbb{E}[|\mathfrak{W}_{s}-\mathfrak{W}_{t}|^{2}\mid\mathfrak{X}]=\tilde{d}_{\mathfrak{X}}(s,t)\,.

The Brownian plane was defined in
[
CLG14
]
as

\big(\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muM\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{W}},\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{W}}\big)=\big(\mathbb{R}/\{\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{W}}=0\},\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{W}}\big)\qquad\text{ where }\qquad\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{W}}=d_{\mathfrak{W}}/\big\{\tilde{d}_{\mathfrak{X}}=0\big\}\,.

The following proposition shows that the definition given in Section
6.3
is equivalent to the one above. Recall the piece of notation
\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(0\wedge t,0\vee t)
and define the process
(\Pi_{t}=X_{t}-2\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t},t\in\mathbb{R})
by taking the Pitman transform of
X
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and on
\mathbb{R}_{-}
.

Proposition 60
.

The process
(\Pi,W)
considered under
\mathbf{Plane}
has same distribution as
(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{W})
defined above. Moreover,

\big(\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muM\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\Pi,W},\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\Pi,W}=d_{W}/\big\{\tilde{d}_{\Pi}=0\big\}\big)
and

(M_{X,W},D_{X,W})
are a.s. equal as metric spaces.

Proof.

We claim that
\tilde{d}_{\Pi}=d_{X}
. This entails that, conditionally
given
X
, the process
W
is also Gaussian with
\mathbb{E}[(W_{s}-W_{t})^{2}\mid X]=\tilde{d}_{\Pi}(s,t)
and, since
\Pi
has same distribution as
\mathfrak{X}
by
Pitman’s
2M-X
theorem
[
Pit75
, Theorem 1.3]
, we see that
(\Pi,W)
and
(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{W})
have same
distribution. We then have

\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\Pi,W}=d_{W}/\big\{\tilde{d}_{\Pi}=0\big\}=d_{W}/\{d_{X}=0\}=D_{X,W}\,.

Checking that
\tilde{d}_{\Pi}=d_{X}
is a classical exercise, based on the fact that, for
0\leq s<t
,

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Pi\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{s}-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}\,,\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\inf_{u\geq s}\Pi_{u}=-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{s}\,.

(56)

The right equation is obtained from the left one by letting

t\to\infty
, noting that, for
t
large enough,

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)=\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}
. The left equation comes from a straightforward
case analysis. If
\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{s}=\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}
, then, for all
u\in[s,t]
,

\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{u}=\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{s}=\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}
and so
\Pi_{u}=X_{u}-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{s}-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}
;
taking the infimum on
u\in[s,t]
gives the result. If

\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{s}>\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}
, then
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)=\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}
so the right-hand
side is
-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{s}
. Let
r\in[s,t]
be such that

X_{r}=\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{r}=\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{s}
. We have
\Pi_{r}=X_{r}-2\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{r}=-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{s}
and,
for
u\geq s
,
\Pi_{u}=X_{u}-2\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{u}\geq-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{u}\geq-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{s}
.

For
0\leq s<t
, the left equation of (
56
) entails

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{\Pi}(t,s)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{s}+\Pi_{t}-2\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Pi\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)

\displaystyle=X_{s}-2\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{s}+X_{t}-2\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}-2\big(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{s}-\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}\big)=d_{X}(s,t)\,.

For
s<0<t
, we have that

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Pi\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(t,s)=\inf_{u\geq t}\Pi_{u}\,\wedge\,\inf_{u\leq s}\Pi_{u}=-\big(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(0,t)\vee\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,0)\big)=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,t)-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(s,0)-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(0,t)

and we conclude as above. The remaining case
s<t<0
is treated similarly.
∎

A.2
Convergence of the UIPQ to the Brownian plane

We use here the setting of Section
6.4
. The proof of Proposition
49
will follow similar lines as that of Proposition
34
, using the coupling results of
[
CLG14
]
. As the law of
\mathfrak{X}
is obtained from that of
X
by taking the Pitman transform on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and on
\mathbb{R}_{-}
, the same should be done for the contour process
C
of the tree
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
. We thus define the process
(\mathfrak{C}(t)=C(t)-2\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muC\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu(t),t\in\mathbb{R})
.

Note that this gives an alternate natural contour process since, for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, it holds that

\mathfrak{C}(i)=d_{\mathbf{T}^{\Upsilon(i)}}\big(v_{i},\rho^{|\Upsilon(i)|}\big)+|\Upsilon(i)|=d_{\mathbf{T}_{\infty}}(v_{i},\rho^{0})\,.

Figure 18:
Left.
Representation of the infinite tree from Figure
16
after moving the trees in such a way that, for
k\geq 0
,
\rho^{k}
is located at
(0,-k)
with
\mathbf{T}^{k}
grafted on its right and
\mathbf{T}^{-k}
on its left.
Top right.
Taking the Pitman transform of the contour process on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and on
\mathbb{R}_{-}
yields an alternate contour process. The differing parts are represented with dot-dashed lines; they correspond to the edges of the infinite spine of the tree. Visually, the process
C
records the height of a particle moving at speed one around the tree when represented as on the left.
Bottom right.
Representation of the tree from Figure
16
where the root of
\mathbf{T}^{k}
is now located at
(k,|k|)
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Note that, in this representation, the roots of
\mathbf{T}^{-k}
and
\mathbf{T}^{k}
differ so that the spine is duplicated. The process
\mathfrak{C}
records the height of a particle moving at speed one around this bi-infinite tree.

In this setting of discrete trees, the Pitman transform on the contour process is very visual: it merely consists of going from reading the trees while moving
down
between trees to reading them while moving
up
between trees; see Figure
18
for an illustration. We may now proceed to the proof of the convergence of the UIPQ to the Brownian plane.

Proof of Proposition
49
.

Similarly as in the proof of Proposition
34
, we fix some number
K>0
and will sample a large plane quadrangulation such that, its properly scaled version and its limit, the Brownian sphere, are indistinguishable from the rescaled UIPQ and the Brownian plane, in a neighborhood of
0
of amplitude
K
. We use again a superscript prime symbol
\prime
for the objects related to the plane quadrangulation and its limit. Here, some care will also be needed when taking an inverse Pitman transform, since this operation a priori involves more than just a neighborhood of
0
.

We fix
L>0
and
n\geq 1
, and consider a
uniform random element

(M^{\prime}_{n},\lambda^{\prime}_{n})
of
\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[0]}_{a_{n},\varnothing}
, where

a_{n}=\lfloor nL\rfloor
, that is,
M^{\prime}_{n}
is a uniform rooted plane
tree with
a_{n}
edges, which we view as a map with a unique face

f_{*}
, and
\lambda^{\prime}_{n}
is a labeling function uniformly distributed among those yielding a well-labeled tree. We let

(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{n},\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}_{n})
be the contour and label function of this tree,
we let
Q^{\prime}_{n}=\CVS(M^{\prime}_{n},\lambda^{\prime}_{n};f_{*})
be the
quadrangulation encoded by
(M^{\prime}_{n},\lambda^{\prime}_{n})
, and we set

\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{n}(i,j)=d_{Q_{n}^{\prime}}(v_{i},v_{j})
for
0\leq i,j\leq 2a_{n}
, where
v_{i}
is
the
i
-th visited vertex in
M^{\prime}_{n}
in contour order, starting from
the root corner, and viewed as a vertex of
Q_{n}^{\prime}
. We extend
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{n}

into a continuous function on
[0,2a_{n}]^{2}
by bilinearity, and all
processes
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{n}
,
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}_{n}
,
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{n}
to
[-2a_{n},2a_{n}]
by the same
formulas as (
36
) and (
37
) but with
l_{n}=0
. We also
define the rescaled versions
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{(n)}
,
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}_{(n)}
,
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{(n)}

exactly as in (
38
). The joint convergence

\big(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{(n)},\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}_{(n)},\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{(n)}\big)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits_{n\to\infty}^{(d)}}\big(\mathfrak{X}^{\prime},\mathfrak{W}^{\prime},\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}\big)

(57)

on the space
\mathcal{C}([-L,L])\times\mathcal{C}([-L,L])\times\mathcal{C}([-L,L]^{2})
is
then a consequence of
[
Mie13
, Theorem 3]
, where the limit is as follows. Restricted to
[0,L]
, the process
\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}
is a
Brownian excursion of duration
L
and
\mathfrak{W}^{\prime}
is the random snake driven by
\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}
, while
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
is a random pseudometric, which
is an explicit function of
(\mathfrak{X}^{\prime},\mathfrak{W}^{\prime})
. Moreover, all these processes are extended to
[-L,L]
by a simple
translation of their argument by
L
.

Let us now recall the relevant aspects of the coupling results of

[
CLG14
]
, between the pairs
(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{W})
and

(\mathfrak{X}^{\prime},\mathfrak{W}^{\prime})
. It will be convenient to let

\bar{\mathfrak{T}}_{x}=\inf\{t\leq 0:\mathfrak{X}_{t}=x\}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{T}_{x}=\sup\{t\geq 0:\mathfrak{X}_{t}=x\}\,.

Fix
r>0
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then by
[
CLG14
, Lemmas 5 and 6]
, it is possible to find
A>1
and then
\alpha>0
and

L_{0}>0
large, such that for
L>L_{0}

the two processes
(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{W})
and
(\mathfrak{X}^{\prime},\mathfrak{W}^{\prime})
, can be
coupled in such a way that on some event
\mathcal{F}
of probability

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\geq 1-\varepsilon
, the following properties hold.

•

For every
s
,
t\in[-\alpha,\alpha]
, one has

\mathfrak{X}_{t}=\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}_{t}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{W}_{t}=\mathfrak{W}^{\prime}_{t}\,.

(58)

•

It holds that

-\alpha<\bar{\mathfrak{T}}_{A^{4}}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathfrak{T}_{A^{4}}<\alpha\,.

(59)

•

For every
s
,
t\in[\bar{\mathfrak{T}}_{A},\mathfrak{T}_{A}]
, the two conditions

\max\big(\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{W}}(0,t),\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{W}}(0,s)\big)\leq r
and
\max(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}(0,t),\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}(0,s))\leq r
are equivalent, and, if these are
satisfied, one has

\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}(s,t)=\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{W}}(s,t)\,.

This choice of coupling being fixed, let us now define
(X_{t},t\in\mathbb{R})

as the unique process such that
\mathfrak{X}_{t}=X_{t}-2\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muX\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu_{t}
is the Pitman
transform of
X
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and on
\mathbb{R}_{-}
; more explicitly

X_{t}=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{X}_{t}-2\inf_{s\geq t}\mathfrak{X}_{s}&\text{ if }t\geq 0\\
\mathfrak{X}_{t}-2\inf_{s\leq t}\mathfrak{X}_{s}&\text{ if }t<0\end{cases}.

Let us also define
W=\mathfrak{W}
. Then
X
indeed has the law of a two-sided
Brownian motion, and
W
is the random snake driven by
X
, so that
(X,W)
has law
\mathbf{Plane}
.
Note that, in this particular coupling, we have
\bar{T}_{x}=\bar{\mathfrak{T}}_{x}
and
T_{x}=\mathfrak{T}_{x}
for every
x\geq 0
, and also
D_{X,W}=\mkern 1.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{W}}
, by the observation in the proof of Proposition
60
.
Moreover, on the event
\mathcal{F}
, the restriction
X{\raisebox{-1.49994pt}{$|$}}{}_{[\bar{T}_{A^{2}},T_{A^{2}}]}
is actually a function of
\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}{\raisebox{-1.49994pt}{$|$}}{}_{[-\alpha,\alpha]}
. Indeed, by (
58
) and (
59
),

X_{t}=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}_{t}-2\inf_{t\leq s\leq\alpha}\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}_{s}&\text{ if }0\leq t\leq T_{A^{2}}\\
\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}_{t}-2\inf_{-\alpha\leq s\leq t}\mathfrak{X}^{\prime}_{s}&\text{ if }\bar{T}_{A^{2}}\leq t<0\end{cases},

since, for
0\leq t\leq T_{A^{2}}
, one has
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathfrak{X}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(t,\infty)=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathfrak{X}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(t,T_{A^{2}})=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathfrak{X}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(t,\alpha)
, and similarly in negative times.

By choosing appropriately the values of
r
, and enlarging the
values of
A
and
\alpha
if necessary, then, similarly to the proof of
Proposition
34
, we obtain that (
58
) holds on

[-K,K]
, and that the restrictions to
[-K,K]^{2}
of
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
and
D_{X,W}
coincide
with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
.

Next, keeping
K
,
\varepsilon
fixed, and possibly up to choosing
L
even
larger, we need to couple the processes

(C_{(n)},\Lambda_{(n)},D_{(n)})
and

(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{(n)},\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}_{(n)},\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{(n)})
appropriately.
To this end, we use the techniques of
[
CLG14
, Proposition 9]
. The latter states that for
\varepsilon>0
, there exists

\alpha>0
(independent of the choice of
L
arising in the definition
of the scaling constant
a_{n}
) such that for every
n
large enough, one may
couple the quadrangulations

Q^{\prime}_{n}
and
Q_{\infty}
in such a way that, with probability at
least
1-\varepsilon
, the balls of radius
\alpha a_{n}^{1/4}
around the root
of
Q^{\prime}_{n}
and
Q_{\infty}
are isometric. The proof proceeds by
coupling the encoding labeled trees
(M^{\prime}_{n},\lambda^{\prime}_{n})
,
and
(\mathbf{T}_{\infty},\lambda_{\infty})
in such a way
that, with even larger probability, the first
\lfloor\delta a_{n}^{1/2}\rfloor
generations of these trees coincide, for some

\delta>0
, and the minimal value of
\lambda_{\infty}
taken on the
vertices
\rho^{0}
,
\rho^{1}
, …,
\rho^{\lfloor\delta a_{n}^{1/2}\rfloor}

of
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}

is less than
-4\alpha a_{n}^{1/4}
. By choosing
R
and then
L
large enough in the
first place, for our choice of
K
, we may also require that with probability at least

1-\varepsilon
,

•

the contour and label processes
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}_{n}
,
\mathfrak{L}^{\prime}_{n}
of
(M^{\prime}_{n},\lambda^{\prime}_{n})

and
\mathfrak{C}
,
\mathfrak{L}
of
(\mathbf{T}_{\infty},\lambda_{\infty})
on the interval
[-2nK,2nK]
involve
only vertices of generations less than
\lfloor Rn^{1/2}\rfloor
, and

•

the most recent common ancestor of the vertices at generation
\lfloor\delta a_{n}^{1/2}\rfloor
has generation at least
\lfloor Rn^{1/2}\rfloor
.

In particular, on this event, the restriction of the
process
C_{n}^{\prime}
to
[-2nK,2nK]
is equal to the restriction of the
process
C
on this same event – in words, the second itemized event
means that the spine of
\mathbf{T}_{\infty}
is determined by the data of

M^{\prime}_{n}
up to generation
Rn^{1/2}
. Since the process
C
is the
inverse Pitman transform of
\mathfrak{C}
, it is then a simple exercise to
conclude that
(C^{\prime}_{(n)},\Lambda^{\prime}_{(n)},D^{\prime}_{(n)})
, which coincides
with
(C_{(n)},\Lambda_{(n)},D_{(n)})
on
[-K,K]
with high probability,
converges to some
(X^{\prime},W^{\prime},D^{\prime})
, which coincides with
(X,W,D_{X,W})
on
[-K,K]

with high
probability.
∎

Appendix B
Scaling limit of size parameters in labeled maps

B.1
Preliminaries

In this appendix, we prove Proposition
24
, following the
method of
[
Bet10
, Proposition 7]
and

[
Bet15
, Proposition 7]
. In the meantime, we obtain an
asymptotic enumeration result for
{\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{Q}}}}}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}^{[g]}
in
Proposition
61
below, which will also allow us to
deduce Theorem
5
and Corollary
6
from Theorem
1
.

Recall that
(g,k)\notin\{(0,0),(0,1)\}
, that
{\boldsymbol{L}}=(L^{1},\dots,L^{k})
is a fixed
k
-tuple such that
L^{1}
, …,
L^{b}>0
, while
L^{b+1}
, …,
L^{k}=0
, and that we consider a fixed sequence of
k
-tuples
{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}=(l_{n}^{1},\dots,l_{n}^{k})\in(\mathbb{Z}_{+})^{k}
,
n\geq 1
, such that
l_{n}^{i}/{\sqrt{2n}}\to L^{i}
as
n\to\infty
, for
1\leq i\leq k
.

We furthermore assume that
n
sufficiently large so that
l_{n}^{i}\geq 1
for each
i\leq b
. We denote by
\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}
the set of rooted genus
g
schemes with
k
holes, such that
\mathlcal{h}_{1}
, …,
\mathlcal{h}_{b}
are faces. Note that our assumption on
n
ensures that
S_{n}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}
.

“Free” parameters and notation.

For every scheme
\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}
, not necessarily dominant, we arbitrarily fix, once and for all, half-edges
\epsilon_{0}\in\vec{I}(\mathbf{s})
, and
\epsilon_{i}\in\vec{B}_{i}(\mathbf{s})
, for
1\leq i\leq b
. We fix an orientation
I(\mathbf{s})
of
\vec{I}(\mathbf{s})
that contains
\epsilon_{0}
and we set
I^{\prime}(\mathbf{s})=I(\mathbf{s})\setminus\{\epsilon_{0}\}
. We also let
v_{0}
be the root vertex of
\mathbf{s}
, and
V^{\prime}(\mathbf{s})=V(\mathbf{s})\setminus\{v_{0}\}
, as in Section
4.2
. Finally, we set

\displaystyle\vec{B}_{0}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{b+1\leq i\leq k}\vec{B}_{i}(\mathbf{s})\,,

\displaystyle\vec{B}_{+}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{1\leq i\leq b}\vec{B}_{i}(\mathbf{s})\,,

\displaystyle\vec{B}^{\prime}_{i}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\vec{B}_{i}(\mathbf{s})\setminus\{\epsilon_{i}\}\,,\text{ for }1\leq i\leq b\,,

\displaystyle\vec{B}^{\prime}_{+}(\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{1\leq i\leq b}\vec{B}^{\prime}_{i}(\mathbf{s})\,.

The motivation for introducing
\vec{B}_{+}
and
\vec{B}_{0}
is that we need a different treatment depending whether the hole perimeters are in the scale
\sqrt{n}
or
\scalebox{.5}[.62]{$\mathbfcal{O}$}(\sqrt{n})
. The sets with a prime symbol should be thought of as the sets containing the parameters on which there is a “degree of freedom.” (
The reason for removing one element from
I
will become clear in a moment. We will not need a
\vec{B}^{\prime}_{0}
since the corresponding perimeters are all asymptotically null in the scale
\sqrt{n}
of interest.
).

From now on, we use the shorthand piece of notation
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\mathcal{E}}
for a family
(x^{j})_{j\in\mathcal{E}}
indexed by a set
\mathcal{E}
. For any subset
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{E}
, we also denote by
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\mathcal{F}}=(x^{j})_{j\in\mathcal{F}}
the subfamily indexed by
\mathcal{F}
, and, in the case of real nonnegative numbers, by
\|{\boldsymbol{x}}\|_{\mathcal{F}}=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{F}}x^{j}
(note in particular that
\|{\boldsymbol{x}}\|_{\varnothing}=0
).

Counting scheme-rooted labeled maps with given size parameters.

For the time being, we do not take the areas parameters into account. We fix a rooted scheme
\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}
, and size parameters
{\boldsymbol{h}}^{\vec{I}(\mathbf{s})}
,
{\boldsymbol{l}}^{\vec{B}(\mathbf{s})}
and
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{V(\mathbf{s})}
. We say that a labeled map is
scheme-rooted on
\mathbf{s}
if its scheme carries an extra root and the scheme rooted at this extra root is
\mathbf{s}
. We consider the elements of
\smash{\mathbf{M}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}}}
scheme-rooted on
\mathbf{s}
whose size parameters are
{\boldsymbol{h}}^{\vec{I}(\mathbf{s})}
,
{\boldsymbol{l}}^{\vec{B}(\mathbf{s})}
and
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{V(\mathbf{s})}
. Reasoning as in Lemmas
9
and
11
, we can express the number of such elements as

12^{n-\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{h}}\|}{2}}2^{\|{\boldsymbol{h}}\|+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}\|}\,Q_{\|{\boldsymbol{h}}\|+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}\|}(2n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}\|)\,\prod_{e\in I(\mathbf{s})}3^{h^{e}}M_{h^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\prod_{e\in\vec{B}(\mathbf{s})}2^{2l^{e}+\delta\!\lambda^{e}}P_{l^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\,,

the products over
I(\mathbf{s})
and
\vec{B}(\mathbf{s})
respectively counting the number of ways to label the vertices along the edges of
I(\mathbf{s})
and
\vec{B}(\mathbf{s})
, and the remaining term counting the labeled forests, which can be seen as one big labeled forest obtained by concatenating all the labeled forests indexed by the half-edges of
\vec{E}(\mathbf{s})
. After recalling that
\sum_{e\in\vec{B}_{i}(\mathbf{s})}\delta\!\lambda^{e}=0
and that
\|{\boldsymbol{l}}\|_{\vec{B}_{i}(\mathbf{s})}=l^{i}
for every
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
corresponding to an external face of
\mathbf{s}
, we may
recast this quantity as

12^{n}\,8^{\|{\boldsymbol{l}}\|}\,Q_{\|{\boldsymbol{h}}\|+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}\|}(2n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}\|)\prod_{e\in I(\mathbf{s})}M_{h^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\prod_{e\in\vec{B}(\mathbf{s})}P_{l^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\,.

Consequently, the number of elements of
\smash{\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}}}
scheme-rooted on
\mathbf{s}
(these labeled maps are thus rooted twice) whose size parameters are
{\boldsymbol{h}}^{\vec{I}(\mathbf{s})}
,
{\boldsymbol{l}}^{\vec{B}(\mathbf{s})}
and
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{V(\mathbf{s})}
is equal to

\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathrm{S}}}}_{n}^{\mathbf{s}}({\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{l}},{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})=(2n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}\|)\,12^{n}\,8^{\|{\boldsymbol{l}}\|}\,Q_{\|{\boldsymbol{h}}\|+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}\|}(2n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}\|)\prod_{e\in I(\mathbf{s})}M_{h^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\prod_{e\in\vec{B}(\mathbf{s})}P_{l^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\,,

(60)

since there are
2n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}\|
possible rootings of the map.

Counting rooted labeled maps.

Next, for
n
,
h\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}
, we set

\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)=\sum_{\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)}\prod_{e\in I(\mathbf{s})}M_{h^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\prod_{e\in\vec{B}(\mathbf{s})}P_{l^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\,,

(61)

where the sum is taken over the set
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)
of all size parameters from labeled maps in
\mathbf{M}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}
scheme-rooted on
\mathbf{s}
, having
h
edges in total on the internal edges of
\mathbf{s}
. More precisely, it is the set of tuples

\Big({\boldsymbol{h}}^{\vec{I}(\mathbf{s})},{\boldsymbol{l}}^{\vec{B}(\mathbf{s})},{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{V(\mathbf{s})}\Big)\in\mathbb{N}^{\vec{I}(\mathbf{s})}\times\mathbb{N}^{\vec{B}(\mathbf{s})}\times\mathbb{Z}^{V(\mathbf{s})}

such that

•

\|{\boldsymbol{h}}\|=2h
,

•

h^{\bar{e}}=h^{e}
, for all
e\in\vec{I}(\mathbf{s})
,

•

\|{\boldsymbol{l}}\|_{\vec{B}_{i}(\mathbf{s})}=l_{n}^{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq k
,

•

\lambda^{v_{0}}=0
.

Note that the conditions
\|{\boldsymbol{l}}\|_{\vec{B}_{i}(\mathbf{s})}=l_{n}^{i}
may only be satisfied if
{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}
is
compatible
with
\mathbf{s}
in the sense that
l_{n}^{i}=0\iff\vec{B}_{i}(\mathbf{s})=\varnothing
for all
i
. As a result,
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)=\varnothing
and thus
\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)=0
whenever
{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}
is not compatible with
\mathbf{s}
.

By double counting the elements of
\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}
scheme-rooted on
\mathbf{s}
, we thus obtain that

\big|\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}\big|=12^{n}8^{\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}}\frac{2n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|}{2|E(\mathbf{s})|}\sum_{h\in\mathbb{N}}Q_{2h+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|}(2n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|)\,\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)\,,

(62)

since we sum over
\bigcup_{\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}},h\in\mathbb{N}}\{\mathbf{s}\}\times\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)
the number
\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathrm{S}}}}_{n}^{\mathbf{s}}({\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n},{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})
given by (
60
), divided by the number
2\,|E(\mathbf{s})|
of possible extra rootings on the scheme.

B.2
Asymptotics of the scheme

When we work with a fixed scheme, which will be the case in all but the fourth paragraph, we drop the argument from the sets in the notation in order to ease the reading, thus writing
I
instead of
I(\mathbf{s})
for instance.

Law of the scheme.

Recall that the triple
(M_{n},\lambda_{n},S_{n})
is a rooted, scheme-rooted, labeled map, where
(M_{n},\lambda_{n})
is uniformly distributed over
\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}
, while, conditionally given it,
S_{n}
is rooted by uniformly choosing its root among
\big\{e,\bar{e}\,:\,e\in\vec{I}(S_{n})\cup\vec{B}_{+}(S_{n})\big\}
. Let us fix a rooted scheme
\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}
whose root or its reverse belongs to
\vec{I}\cup\vec{B}_{+}
. Writing
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbf{s}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
the nonrooted scheme corresponding to
\mathbf{s}
, observe that the set of rooted labeled maps in
\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}
with scheme
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbf{s}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
is in bijection with the set of rooted labeled maps in
\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}
scheme-rooted on
\mathbf{s}
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(S_{n}=\mathbf{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{m},\lambda)\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}\\
\text{with scheme }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathbf{s}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\big((M_{n},\lambda_{n})=(\mathbf{m},\lambda),\,S_{n}=\mathbf{s}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{m},\lambda)\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}\\
\text{scheme-rooted on }\mathbf{s}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\big((M_{n},\lambda_{n})=(\mathbf{m},\lambda)\big)\,\mathbb{P}\big(S_{n}=\mathbf{s}\mid(M_{n},\lambda_{n})=(\mathbf{m},\lambda)\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)}\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathrm{S}}}}_{n}^{\mathbf{s}}({\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n},{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})\frac{1}{\big|\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}\big|}\frac{1}{|\vec{I}\,|+2\,|\vec{B}_{+}|}=\frac{\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(n)}{\mathcal{Z}_{1}(n)}\,,

where

\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(n)=\frac{1}{|\vec{I}(\mathbf{s})|+2\,|\vec{B}_{+}(\mathbf{s})|}\sum_{h\in\mathbb{N}}Q_{2h+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|}(2n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|)\,\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)

(63)

and
\mathcal{Z}_{1}(n)=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}}\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(n)
is the proper normalization constant.

Schemes with tadpoles.

Here, we fix a scheme
\mathbf{s}
whose external faces among
\mathlcal{h}_{i}
,
b+1\leq i\leq k
, are all tadpoles. Equivalently, each
\vec{B}_{i}
,
b+1\leq i\leq k
, is either empty or a singleton. In this case, by the Euler characteristic formula,

|V^{\prime}|-|I|-|\vec{B}^{\prime}_{+}|=-2g\,,

(64)

since
\mathbf{s}
has
|I|+|\vec{B}^{\prime}_{+}|+b+|\vec{B}_{0}|
edges and
1+b+|\vec{B}_{0}|
faces.

Assuming that
{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}
is compatible with
\mathbf{s}
, we write the sum over
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)
in (
61
) as an integral under the Lebesgue measure

\mathrm{d}\mathrm{L}_{\mathbf{s}}=\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{h}}^{I^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{l}}^{\vec{B}^{\prime}_{+}}\otimes\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{V^{\prime}}\quad\text{ over }\quad(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{I^{\prime}}\times(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{\vec{B}^{\prime}_{+}}\times\mathbb{R}^{V^{\prime}}\,

and obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)

\displaystyle=\prod_{e\in\vec{B}_{0}}P_{l^{e}}(0)\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{L}_{\mathbf{s}}\,\prod_{e\in I}M_{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{e}}(\delta\!\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\lambda\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{e})\prod_{e\in\vec{B}_{+}}P_{\underline{l}^{e}}(\delta\!\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\lambda\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{e})\,,

(65)

where
l^{e}=l_{n}^{i}
if
e
is the unique element of
\vec{B}_{i}
, for
i>b
, and

•

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{e}=\lceil h^{e}\rceil
, for
e\in I^{\prime}
,

•

\underline{l}^{e}=\lceil l^{e}\rceil
, for
e\in\vec{B}^{\prime}_{+}
,

•

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\lambda\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{v}=\lceil\lambda^{v}\rceil
, for
v\in V^{\prime}
,

•

\smash{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\epsilon_{0}}=h-\sum\limits_{e\in I^{\prime}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{e}}
,

•

\smash{\underline{l}^{\epsilon_{i}}=l_{n}^{i}-\sum\limits_{e\in\vec{B}^{\prime}_{i}}\underline{l}^{e}}
, for
1\leq i\leq b
,

•

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\lambda\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{v_{0}}=0
.

(
Note that the ceiling function is superfluous for integer parameters; we kept it for notational simplicity.
) In order to deal with the cases where
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\epsilon_{0}}\leq 0
or
\underline{l}^{\epsilon_{i}}\leq 0
, we simply declare
16
16

16

This is just a convenience. Note that we set
M_{0}(0)=P_{0}(0)=0
here, although it would be more natural from a combinatorial point of view to set both these quantities to
1
.

M_{\ell}(j)=P_{\ell}(j)=0
whenever
\ell\leq 0
.

Observe that
{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}
compatible with
\mathbf{s}
means that
\vec{B}_{0}
corresponds to
\{i>b:l^{i}_{n}\geq 1\}
.
We then make the changes of variables in the natural scales to obtain

\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)=3^{\frac{b}{2}-g}\,2^{\frac{|V^{\prime}|}{2}-\frac{g}{2}-\frac{3}{4}b}\,n^{\frac{|V^{\prime}|}{2}+\frac{g}{2}-\frac{b}{4}-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{i>b\,:\,l^{i}_{n}\geq 1}P_{l^{i}_{n}}(0)\\
\times\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{L}_{\mathbf{s}}\,\prod_{e\in I}\Big(\frac{8n}{9}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}M_{\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{e}}(\delta\!\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\lambda\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{e})\prod_{e\in\vec{B}_{+}}\Big(\frac{8n}{9}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}P_{\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{l}}}}^{e}}(\delta\!\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\lambda\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{e})\,,

(66)

where

•

\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{e}=\lceil\sqrt{2n}\,h^{e}\rceil
, for
e\in I^{\prime}
,

•

\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{l}}}}^{e}=\lceil\sqrt{2n}\,l^{e}\rceil
, for
e\in\vec{B}^{\prime}_{+}
,

•

\smash{\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\lambda\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{v}=\lceil(\tfrac{8n}{9})^{\frac{1}{4}}\lambda^{v}\rceil}
, for
v\in V^{\prime}
,

•

\smash{\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{\epsilon_{0}}=h-\sum\limits_{e\in I^{\prime}}\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{e}}
,

•

\smash{\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{l}}}}^{\epsilon_{i}}=l_{n}^{i}-\sum\limits_{e\in\vec{B}^{\prime}_{i}}\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{l}}}}^{e}}
, for
1\leq i\leq b
,

•

\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\lambda\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{v_{0}}=0
.

We finally use the same method to treat the summation over
h\in\mathbb{N}
in (
63
), that is, we see it as an integral and do the proper change of variables. We write
{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\bowtie\mathbf{s}
to mean “
{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}
compatible with
\mathbf{s}
”:

\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(n)=\frac{\mathbf{1}_{{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\bowtie\mathbf{s}}}{|\vec{I}\,|+|\vec{B}_{+}|}\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\mathrm{d}\mathsf{h}\,nQ_{2\lceil\sqrt{2n}\,\mathsf{h}\rceil+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|}(2n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|)\,\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}\big(\lceil\sqrt{2n}\,\mathsf{h}\rceil,n\big)\,.

(67)

Setting
h^{\epsilon_{0}}=\mathsf{h}-\sum_{e\in I^{\prime}}h^{e}
,
l^{\epsilon_{i}}=L^{i}-\sum_{e\in\vec{B}^{\prime}_{i}}l^{e}
for
1\leq i\leq b
, and
\lambda^{v_{0}}=0
, by the local limit theorem
[
Pet75
, Theorem VII.1.6]
, it holds that, when
h\sim\sqrt{2n}\,\mathsf{h}
,

\Big(\frac{8n}{9}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}M_{\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{e}}(\delta\!\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\lambda\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{e})\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits_{n\to\infty}}p_{h^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\,,\qquad\qquad\Big(\frac{8n}{9}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}P_{\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{l}}}}^{e}}(\delta\!\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\lambda\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{e})\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits_{n\to\infty}}p_{3l^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\,,

(68)

and

nQ_{2\lceil\sqrt{2n}\,\mathsf{h}\rceil+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|}(2n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits_{n\to\infty}}q_{2\mathsf{h}+\|{\boldsymbol{L}}\|}(1)\,.

(69)

Consequently, provided the domination hypothesis obtained in the following paragraph, we get the following equivalent:

\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(n){\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\sim}\limits_{n\to\infty}}}c_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}({\boldsymbol{L}})\,\mathbf{1}_{{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\bowtie\mathbf{s}}\,n^{\frac{|V^{\prime}|}{2}+\frac{g}{2}-\frac{b}{4}-1}\prod_{i>b\,:\,l^{i}_{n}\geq 1}P_{l^{i}_{n}}(0)

(70)

where the constant
c_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}({\boldsymbol{L}})
is given by

c_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}({\boldsymbol{L}})=\frac{1}{|\vec{I}\,|+2\,|\vec{B}_{+}|}3^{\frac{b}{2}-g}\,2^{\frac{|V^{\prime}|}{2}-\frac{g}{2}-\frac{3}{4}b+\frac{1}{2}}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\!\mathrm{d}\mathsf{h}\,q_{2\mathsf{h}+\|{\boldsymbol{L}}\|}(1)\,\int\!\mathrm{d}\mathrm{L}_{\mathbf{s}}\,\prod_{e\in I}p_{h^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\prod_{e\in\vec{B}_{+}}p_{3l^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\,.

Domination hypothesis.

In order to show that the convergence is dominated, we use the bounds of Petrov
[
Pet75
, Theorem VII.3.16]
, stating that there exist a constant
C
such that, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
j\in\mathbb{Z}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and
r\in\mathbb{N}
,

M_{\ell}(j)\vee P_{\ell}(j)\leq C\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\ell}}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad Q_{i}(\ell)\leq C\,\frac{i}{\ell^{3/2}}\frac{1}{1+({i^{2}}/{\ell})^{r}}\,.

(71)

We fix an arbitrary spanning tree of
\mathbf{s}
, that is, a tree with vertex-set
V
and edge-set a subset of
E
. We associate with any vertex
v\neq v_{0}
the first edge of the unique path in the tree from
v
to
v_{0}
and we denote by
e_{v}
the unique half-edge of
I\cup\vec{B}
that corresponds to this edge.

We bound the integrand in (
66
) as follows. First, by (
71
), we have, for
e\in I^{\prime}
,

\Big(\frac{8n}{9}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}M_{\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{e}}(\delta\!\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\lambda\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{e})\leq\Big(\frac{8n}{9}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}\frac{C}{\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{e}}}\leq\Big(\frac{8n}{9}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}\frac{C}{\sqrt{\sqrt{2n}\,h^{e}}}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\frac{C}{\sqrt{h^{e}}}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{h^{e}}}\,.

For
h=\lceil\sqrt{2n}\,\mathsf{h}\rceil
and
h^{\epsilon_{0}}=\mathsf{h}-\sum_{e\in I^{\prime}}h^{e}
, a similar bound holds for
e=\epsilon_{0}
, up to possibly enlarging the constant. Indeed, it suffices to show that
\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{\epsilon_{0}}
is bounded from below by a constant times
\sqrt{2n}\,h^{\epsilon_{0}}
in order to complete the computation. We may assume that
\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{\epsilon_{0}}\geq 1
as otherwise the left-hand side is null. Then, if
\sqrt{2n}\,h^{\epsilon_{0}}\leq 2{|I^{\prime}|}
, it immediately holds that
\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{\epsilon_{0}}\geq\frac{1}{2|I^{\prime}|}\sqrt{2n}\,h^{\epsilon_{0}}
. Otherwise,

\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{\epsilon_{0}}=h-\sum\limits_{e\in I^{\prime}}\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{e}\geq\sqrt{2n}\,h^{\epsilon_{0}}-|I^{\prime}|\geq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2n}\,h^{\epsilon_{0}}\,.

In conclusion, up to changing the constant
C
, it holds that, for all
e\in I
,

\Big(\frac{8n}{9}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}M_{\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{e}}(\delta\!\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\lambda\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{e})\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{h^{e}}}\,.

Similarly, up to enlarging the constant
C
even more, setting
l^{\epsilon_{i}}=L^{i}-\sum_{e\in\vec{B}^{\prime}_{i}}l^{e}
for
1\leq i\leq b
, it holds that, for
e\in\vec{B}_{+}
,

\Big(\frac{8n}{9}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}P_{\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{l}}}}^{e}}(\delta\!\mkern 1.0mu\smash{\underline{\vphantom{o_{o}}\smash{\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\lambda\mkern-1.0mu}}}}\mkern 1.0mu^{e})\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{l^{e}}}\,.

We use these bounds whenever
e\notin\vec{E}_{V}=\big\{e_{v}:v\in V\setminus\{v_{0}\}\big\}
and then, we operate the integral with respect to
\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{V^{\prime}}
vertices by vertices, starting from a leaf of the fixed spanning tree, then from a leaf of the tree remaining after removing the first vertex, and so on until only
v_{0}
remains. Since, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

\int\mathrm{d}x\,\Big(\frac{8n}{9}\Big)^{\frac{1}{4}}M_{\ell}\big(\lceil(\tfrac{8n}{9})^{\frac{1}{4}}x\rceil\big)=1,

and similarly with
P_{\ell}
instead of
M_{\ell}
, we obtain that, for
n
sufficiently large and after integration with respect to
\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{V^{\prime}}
, the integrand in (
66
) is bounded by

\mathbf{1}_{\{\|{\boldsymbol{h}}\|_{I}\leq 2\mathsf{h}\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{\|{\boldsymbol{l}}\|_{\vec{B}^{\prime}_{+}}\leq 2\|{\boldsymbol{L}}\|\}}\prod_{e\in I\setminus\vec{E}_{V}}\frac{C}{\sqrt{h^{e}}}\prod_{e\in\vec{B}_{+}\setminus\vec{E}_{V}}\frac{C}{\sqrt{l^{e}}}\,.

(72)

This is integrable with respect to
\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{h}}^{I^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{l}}^{\vec{B}^{\prime}_{+}}
and is bounded, after integration, by some constant times some power of
\mathsf{h}
. Taking
r
sufficiently large in (
71
) yields that this quantity multiplied by
nQ_{2\lceil\sqrt{2n}\,\mathsf{h}\rceil+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|}(2n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|)
is integrable with respect to
\mathrm{d}\mathsf{h}
. The claimed dominated convergence follows.

Dominant schemes.

We will now see which schemes are such that
\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(n)
has the highest possible order in
n
. The exponent of
n
in the equivalent (
70
) is maximal when
|V(\mathbf{s})|
is the largest; in this case,

|V(\mathbf{s})|=2(2g+k-1)\,.

This equality is obtained as in the proof of Lemma
7
, since
|V(\mathbf{s})|
being the largest means that the vertices have the lowest possible degrees, namely
3
for the internal vertices and
1
for the external vertices. More precisely, denoting by
v
,
e
,
f
the numbers of vertices, edges and faces of
\mathbf{s}
, as well as
t
the number of tadpoles among
\mathlcal{h}_{b+1}
, …,
\mathlcal{h}_{k}
, we obtain
f=b+t+1
,
2e=3(v-k+b+t)+k-b-t
, and the result from the Euler characteristic formula
v-e+f=2-2g
.

Next, the local limit theorem
[
Pet75
, Theorem VII.1.6]
yields the existence of a compact set
K\subset(0,\infty)
such that, for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\sqrt{\ell}\,P_{\ell}(0)\in K
. Finally, for any
\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}
, we denote by
\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ}
the scheme obtained by shrinking every tadpole among
\mathlcal{h}_{b+1}
, …,
\mathlcal{h}_{k}
into a vertex. For any fixed dominant scheme
\mathbf{d}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}^{\star}
, observe that there exist exactly one scheme among
\{\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}:\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ}=\mathbf{d}\}
that is compatible with
{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}
, namely the one whose tadpoles among
\mathlcal{h}_{b+1}
, …,
\mathlcal{h}_{k}
are the holes indexed by
\{i>b:l_{n}^{i}\geq 1\}
. Furthermore, if
\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}
is such that
\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}^{\star}
, then the external faces among
\mathlcal{h}_{i}
,
b+1\leq i\leq k
, of
\mathbf{s}
are all tadpoles. We may thus use the equivalent (
70
) for these schemes. Consequently, as
n\to\infty
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}\\
\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ}=\mathbf{d}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(n){\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits}}\Theta\Bigg(n^{\frac{5(g-1)}{2}+k-\frac{b}{4}}\prod_{i>b\,:\,l^{i}_{n}\geq 1}(l^{i}_{n})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Bigg)\,.

(73)

In particular, if
\mathbf{s}
has only tadpoles among its external faces indexed by
b+1\leq i\leq k
but is such that
\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ}
is not dominant, then
\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(n)
is negligible with respect to this sum.

Nondominant schemes.

We will now see that the above is the highest order in
n
and that it is only obtained for the schemes that are dominant after the tadpoles shrinkage. To this end, we fix an arbitrary scheme
\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}
. As above, we consider an arbitrary spanning tree of
\mathbf{s}
and still denote by
e_{v}\in I\cup\vec{B}
the half-edge corresponding to
v\in V^{\prime}
, as well as
\vec{E}_{V}=\big\{e_{v}:v\in V^{\prime}\big\}
.

In (
61
), we bound
M_{h^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})
or
P_{l^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})
thanks to (
71
) if
e\notin\vec{E}_{V}
and we operate the sum over
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{V^{\prime}}
leaf by leaf as we did in the previous paragraph. Since, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}M_{\ell}(j)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}P_{\ell}(j)=1
, we obtain the bound

\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)\leq\sum_{{\boldsymbol{h}}^{\vec{I}},\,{\boldsymbol{l}}^{\vec{B}}}\prod_{e\in I\setminus\vec{E}_{V}}\frac{C}{\sqrt{h^{e}}}\prod_{e\in\vec{B}\setminus\vec{E}_{V}}\frac{C}{\sqrt{l^{e}}}\,,

where the sum is over the tuples
{\boldsymbol{h}}^{\vec{I}}
,
{\boldsymbol{l}}^{\vec{B}}
satisfying the conditions of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)
. Seeing the sums as integrals under the simplex Lebesgue measures
\Delta_{I}^{h}
, and
\Delta_{\vec{B}_{i}}^{l^{i}_{n}}
whenever
\vec{B}_{i}\neq\varnothing
yields, after renormalization by
h
or
l^{i}_{n}
, integrals of Dirichlet distributions (with parameter vectors containing only
\frac{1}{2}
’s and
1
’s). As a result,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)

\displaystyle\lesssim h^{|I|-1-\frac{1}{2}|I\setminus\vec{E}_{V}|}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
\vec{B}_{i}\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}(l^{i}_{n})^{|\vec{B}_{i}|-1-\frac{1}{2}|\vec{B}_{i}\setminus\vec{E}_{V}|}\,

\displaystyle\quad=h^{\frac{1}{2}|I|+\frac{1}{2}|I\cap\vec{E}_{V}|-1}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq k\\
\vec{B}_{i}\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}(l^{i}_{n})^{\frac{1}{2}|\vec{B}_{i}|+\frac{1}{2}|\vec{B}_{i}\cap\vec{E}_{V}|-1}\,,

where we used the symbol
\lesssim
to mean bounded up to a constant independent
17
17

17

Recall from Lemma
7
that the number of edges in the schemes from
\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}
is bounded.
of
\mathbf{s}
,
h
, and
n
. Since
l^{i}_{n}
is in the scale
\sqrt{n}
for
i\leq b
, the part of the product concerning these indices is bounded by a constant times

n^{\frac{1}{4}|\vec{B}^{\prime}_{+}|+\frac{1}{4}|\vec{B}_{+}\cap\vec{E}_{V}|-\frac{b}{4}}\,.

Recall that
B_{i}\neq\varnothing\iff l^{i}_{n}\geq 1
when
{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}
is compatible with
\mathbf{s}
. Using (
67
), which is valid for any scheme, as well as the bound (
71
) as above to get integrability, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(n)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbf{1}_{{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\bowtie\mathbf{s}}\,n^{\frac{1}{4}(|I|+|\vec{B}^{\prime}_{+}|)+\frac{1}{4}|(I\cup\vec{B}_{+})\cap\vec{E}_{V}|-\frac{b}{4}-1}\,\prod_{i>b\,:\,l^{i}_{n}\geq 1}(l^{i}_{n})^{\frac{1}{2}|\vec{B}_{i}|+\frac{1}{2}|\vec{B}_{i}\cap\vec{E}_{V}|-1}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbf{1}_{{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\bowtie\mathbf{s}}\,n^{\frac{1}{4}(|I|+|\vec{B}^{\prime}_{+}|+|V^{\prime}|)-\frac{b}{4}-1}\,\prod_{i>b\,:\,l^{i}_{n}\geq 1}(l^{i}_{n})^{\frac{1}{2}|\vec{B}_{i}|-1}\,,

since
l_{n}^{i}=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n})
for all
i
, and
|(I\cup\vec{B}_{+}\cup\vec{B}_{0})\cap\vec{E}_{V}|=|V^{\prime}|
. Using again the Euler characteristic formula, as well as the bound
|V|\leq 2(2g+k-1)
, we obtain

\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(n)\lesssim\mathbf{1}_{{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\bowtie\mathbf{s}}\,n^{\frac{5(g-1)}{2}+k-\frac{b}{4}}\prod_{i>b\,:\,l^{i}_{n}\geq 1}(l^{i}_{n})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\frac{l^{i}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}(|\vec{B}_{i}|-1)}\,,

which gives an order lower than that of (
73
) as soon as there exists
i>b
such that
|\vec{B}_{i}|\geq 2
since
l^{i}_{n}=\scalebox{.5}[.62]{$\mathbfcal{O}$}(\sqrt{n})
. As a result, the normalization constant
\mathcal{Z}_{1}(n)
is of the order appearing in (
73
) and
\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(n)=\scalebox{.5}[.62]{$\mathbfcal{O}$}(\mathcal{Z}_{1}(n))
whenever
\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ}\notin\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}^{\star}
. In particular,
\mathbb{P}(S_{n}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}^{\star})\to 1
as
n\to\infty
and we obtain the first statement of Proposition
24
: with asymptotic probability
1
, every vanishing face of
M_{n}
induces a tadpole in
S_{n}
.

B.3
Asymptotics of the size parameters

Limiting distribution of the size parameters.

Given a bounded continuous function
\phi
on the set

\bigcup_{\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}\,:\,\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ}=\mathbf{s}}\{\mathbf{s}\}\times(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{\vec{I}(\mathbf{s})}\times(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{\vec{B}(\mathbf{s})}\times\mathbb{R}^{V(\mathbf{s})},

we set, for
n
,
h\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}
,

\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}^{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)=\sum_{\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)}\phi\left(\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ},\frac{{\boldsymbol{h}}^{\vec{I}(\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ})}}{\sqrt{2n}},\frac{{\boldsymbol{l}}^{\vec{B}(\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ})}}{\sqrt{2n}},\frac{{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{V(\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ})}}{(8n/9)^{1/4}}\right)\prod_{e\in I(\mathbf{s})}M_{h^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\prod_{e\in\vec{B}(\mathbf{s})}P_{l^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\,,

and

\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}^{\mathbf{s}}(n)=\frac{1}{|\vec{I}(\mathbf{s})|+2\,|\vec{B}_{+}(\mathbf{s})|}\sum_{h\in\mathbb{N}}Q_{2h+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|}(2n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|)\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}^{\mathbf{s}}(h,n)\,,

so that

\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(S_{n}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ},\frac{\mathbf{H}_{n}^{\vec{I}(S_{n}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ})}}{\sqrt{2n}},\frac{{\boldsymbol{L}}^{\vec{B}(S_{n}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ})}_{n}}{\sqrt{2n}},\frac{{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{n}^{V(S_{n}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ})}}{(8n/9)^{1/4}}\right)\right]=\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{1}(n)}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}}\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}^{\mathbf{s}}(n)\,.

Conducting with
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}^{\mathbf{s}}(n)
exactly the same computations as the ones we did with
\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(n)
, we obtain the same domination (up to
\sup|\phi|
) when
\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ}
is not dominant and a similar equivalent when
\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ}
is dominant, namely (
70
) where
c_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}({\boldsymbol{L}})
is replaced with

c_{\phi}^{\mathbf{s}}({\boldsymbol{L}})=\frac{1}{|\vec{I}(\mathbf{s})|+2\,|\vec{B}_{+}(\mathbf{s})|}\,3^{\frac{b}{2}-g}\,2^{\frac{|V^{\prime}(\mathbf{s})|}{2}-\frac{g}{2}-\frac{3}{4}b+\frac{1}{2}}\\
\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}\mathrm{d}\mathsf{h}\,q_{2\mathsf{h}+\|{\boldsymbol{L}}\|}(1)\,\int\mathrm{d}\mathrm{L}_{\mathbf{s}}\,\phi\Big(\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ},{\boldsymbol{h}}^{\vec{I}(\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ})},{\boldsymbol{l}}^{\vec{B}(\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ})},{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}^{V(\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ})}\Big)\prod_{e\in I(\mathbf{s})}p_{h^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\prod_{e\in\vec{B}_{+}(\mathbf{s})}p_{3l^{e}}(\delta\!\lambda^{e})\,.

From the Euler characteristic formula, we obtain that
|\vec{I}(\mathbf{s})|+2\,|\vec{B}_{+}(\mathbf{s})|=2|E(\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ})|=2(6g+2p+b-3)
does not depend on
\mathbf{s}
, and we remind that
|V^{\prime}(\mathbf{s})|=4g+2p-3
does not either. Let us consider a dominant scheme
\mathbf{d}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}^{\star}
and an integer
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We let
\mathbf{d}_{n}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}
be the unique scheme compatible with
{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}
and such that
\mathbf{d}_{n}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ}=\mathbf{d}
. Recall that this is the scheme obtained from
\mathbf{d}
by making into tadpoles the external vertices indexed by
\{i>b:l_{n}^{i}\geq 1\}
. Since the above integral only involves
\mathbf{s}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ}
, we have
c_{\phi}^{\mathbf{d}_{n}}({\boldsymbol{L}})=c_{\phi}^{\mathbf{d}}({\boldsymbol{L}})
, and then

\mathcal{Z}_{1}(n)=\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}}\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(n)\sim\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}^{\star}}\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{d}_{n}}(n)\sim n^{\frac{5(g-1)}{2}+k-\frac{b}{4}}\prod_{i>b\,:\,l^{i}_{n}\geq 1}P_{l^{i}_{n}}(0)\,\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}^{\star}}c_{1}^{\mathbf{d}}({\boldsymbol{L}})\,,

and

\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(S_{n}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ},\frac{\mathbf{H}_{n}^{\vec{I}(S_{n}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ})}}{\sqrt{2n}},\frac{{\boldsymbol{L}}^{\vec{B}(S_{n}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ})}_{n}}{\sqrt{2n}},\frac{{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{n}^{V(S_{n}^{\mathclap{\,\cdot}\circ})}}{(8n/9)^{1/4}}\right)\right]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits_{n\to\infty}}\frac{1}{\sum\limits_{\mathbf{d}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}^{\star}}c_{1}^{\mathbf{d}}({\boldsymbol{L}})}\,\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}^{\star}}c_{\phi}^{\mathbf{d}}({\boldsymbol{L}})\,.

(74)

In passing, we obtain the following asymptotic formula for the cardinality of
{\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{Q}}}}}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}^{[g]}
, which readily yields Proposition
4
(corresponding to the case
k=b
), the unrooting giving a factor
1/4n
coming from (
2
). We define the continuous function in
{\boldsymbol{L}}\in(0,\infty)^{b}

t_{g}({\boldsymbol{L}})=\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}^{\star}}c_{1}^{\mathbf{d}}({\boldsymbol{L}})\,,

and we add the excluded cases
(g,k)=(0,0)
and
(g,k,b)=(0,1,1)
, which are needed in Section
1.5
: we set

t_{0}(\varnothing)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad t_{0}(L)=\frac{2^{-\frac{9}{4}}}{\pi\sqrt{L}}e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{2}}\,.

Proposition 61
.

As
n\to\infty
, it holds that

\big|{\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{Q}}}}}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}^{[g]}\big|\sim 4\,t_{g}({\boldsymbol{L}})\,12^{n}\,8^{\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|}\,n^{\frac{5(g-1)}{2}+k-\frac{b}{4}}\frac{e^{\star}}{e^{\star}+p_{n}^{\lozenge}}\prod_{i>b\,:\,l^{i}_{n}\geq 1}P_{l^{i}_{n}}(0)\,,

where
e^{\star}=6g+2p+b-3
is the common number of edges of all dominant schemes, and
p_{n}^{\lozenge}=|\{i>b:l_{n}^{i}\geq 1\}|
is the number of external faces among
\mathlcal{h}_{b+1}
, …,
\mathlcal{h}_{k}
in the maps of
\mathbf{M}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}
.

In the excluded cases
(g,k)=(0,0)
and
(g,k,b)=(0,1,1)
, a similar formula holds:

\big|{\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{Q}}}}}_{n,\varnothing}^{[0]}\big|\sim 4\,t_{0}(\varnothing)\,12^{n}\,n^{-\frac{5}{2}}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\big|{\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{Q}}}}}_{n,(l_{n})}^{[0]}\big|\sim 4\,t_{0}(L)\,12^{n}\,8^{l_{n}}\,n^{-\frac{7}{4}}

for
L>0
and
l_{n}\sim\sqrt{2n}\,L
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

Recall from Section
2.2
that
\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}
is in one-to-two correspondence with
\smash{\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{Q}}}}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}0}}
, and that (
62
) gives its cardinality. Using (
3
) then (
62
) and (
63
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\big|\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{Q}}}}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}\big|

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|+2-2g-k}\,\big|\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}}\big|

\displaystyle=2\,\frac{2n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|}{n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|+2-2g-k}\,12^{n}8^{\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|}\sum_{\mathbf{s}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}}\frac{|\vec{I}(\mathbf{s})|+2|\vec{B}_{+}(\mathbf{s})|}{2\,|E(\mathbf{s})|}\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{s}}(n)

\displaystyle\sim 4\times 12^{n}\,8^{\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|}\,\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}^{\star}}\frac{|E(\mathbf{d})|}{|E(\mathbf{d}_{n})|}\mathcal{Z}_{1}^{\mathbf{d}_{n}}(n)

\displaystyle\sim 4\times 12^{n}\,8^{\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|}\,n^{\frac{5(g-1)}{2}+k-\frac{b}{4}}\frac{e^{\star}}{e^{\star}+p_{n}^{\lozenge}}\prod_{i>b\,:\,l^{i}_{n}\geq 1}P_{l^{i}_{n}}(0)\,\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{S}}}}^{\star}}c_{1}^{\mathbf{d}}({\boldsymbol{L}})\,,

which gives the desired first statement.

The excluded cases
(g,k)=(0,0)
and
(g,k,b)=(0,1,1)
are standard; they are obtained similarly, by computing
|\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[0]}_{n,\varnothing}\big|
and
|\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[0]}_{n,(l_{n})}\big|
. More precisely, it is well known that

\big|\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[0]}_{n,\varnothing}\big|=3^{n}\frac{(2n)!}{n!(n+1)!}\sim\frac{12^{n}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\,n^{-\frac{3}{2}},.

In order to compute the remaining cardinality, we proceed as in Section
B.1
and obtain

\big|\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[0]}_{n,(l_{n})}\big|=\frac{2n+l_{n}}{l_{n}}\,12^{n}\,8^{l_{n}}\,Q_{l_{n}}(2n+l_{n})P_{l_{n}}(0)\,,

the division by
l_{n}
taking into account the fact that seeing an element of
\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{M}}}}^{[0]}_{n,(l_{n})}
as a forest amounts to choose a first tree among
l_{n}
. From the equivalents (
68
) and (
69
), this yields

\big|{\smash{\overset{\rightarrow}{\vphantom{o}\smash{\mathbf{Q}}}}}_{n,(l_{n})}^{[0]}\big|\sim\frac{2}{n}\,\frac{2n}{\sqrt{2n}\,L}\,12^{n}\,8^{l_{n}}\,\frac{1}{n}q_{L}(1)\,\Big(\frac{8n}{9}\Big)^{-\frac{1}{4}}p_{3L}(0)\,,

which gives the desired result.
∎

Limiting distribution of the areas.

We finally take into account the areas. To this end, observe that, conditionally given

\left(S_{n},{\mathbf{H}_{n}^{\vec{I}(S_{n})}},{{\boldsymbol{L}}^{\vec{B}(S_{n})}_{n}}\right)\,,

the area vector
{\mathbf{A}_{n}^{\vec{E}(S_{n})}}
is distributed as follows. We arrange the half-edges
e_{1}
, …,
e_{\kappa}
incident to the internal face of
S_{n}
according to the contour order, starting arbitrarily, and let
x_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{i}\ell_{j}
, where
\ell_{j}=H_{n}^{e_{j}}
if
e_{j}\in\vec{I}(S_{n})
or
\ell_{j}=L_{n}^{e_{j}}
if
e_{j}\in\vec{B}(S_{n})
. Then,
A_{n}^{e_{1}}
,
A_{n}^{e_{1}}+A_{n}^{e_{2}}
,
A_{n}^{e_{1}}+A_{n}^{e_{2}}+A_{n}^{e_{3}}
, …, are distributed as the hitting times of the successive levels
-x_{1}
,
-x_{1}-x_{2}
,
-x_{1}-x_{2}-x_{3}
, …, by a simple random walk conditioned on hitting the final level
-\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}x_{j}=-\|\mathbf{H}_{n}\|-\|{\boldsymbol{L}}_{n}\|
at time
2n+\|{\boldsymbol{l}}_{n}\|
. The desired convergence (
19
) easily follows from this together with (
74
), as well as the fact that, for every
e\in\vec{B}_{0}(S_{n})
, we have
A^{e}_{n}+L_{n}^{e}=\Theta((L_{n}^{e})^{2})
in probability.

B.4
Boltzmann quadrangulations

We finally prove Theorem
5
; in its setting,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}\left(F\left(\Omega_{a^{-1}}(Q)\right)\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{Q}^{[g]}_{{\boldsymbol{l}}_{a}{\boldsymbol{0}}^{p}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in[a^{-1}\!/K,a^{-1}K]\cap\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\mathcal{W}\left(\mathbf{Q}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{a}{\boldsymbol{0}}^{p}}\right)\mathcal{W}\big[F\big(\Omega_{a^{-1}}(Q)\big)\bigm|\mathbf{Q}^{[g]}_{n,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{a}{\boldsymbol{0}}^{p}}\big]

\displaystyle=a^{-1}\int_{a\lfloor a^{-1}\!/K\rfloor}^{a\lfloor a^{-1}K\rfloor}\mathrm{d}A\>\mathcal{W}\left(\mathbf{Q}^{[g]}_{\lfloor A/a\rfloor,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{a}{\boldsymbol{0}}^{p}}\right)\mathcal{W}\big[F\big(\Omega_{a^{-1}}(Q)\big)\bigm|\mathbf{Q}^{[g]}_{\lfloor A/a\rfloor,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{a}{\boldsymbol{0}}^{p}}\big]\,.

By Theorem
1
and the definition of

\mathsf{S}^{[g]}_{A,{\boldsymbol{L}}}
, it holds that

\mathcal{W}\big[F\big(\Omega_{a^{-1}}(Q)\big)\bigm|\mathbf{Q}^{[g]}_{\lfloor A/a\rfloor,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{a}{\boldsymbol{0}}^{p}}\big]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits_{a\downarrow 0}}\mathbb{E}\big[F\big(\mathsf{S}^{[g]}_{A,{\boldsymbol{L}}{\boldsymbol{0}}^{p}}\big)\big]\,,

while Proposition
4
yields that

\mathcal{W}\left(\mathbf{Q}^{[g]}_{\lfloor A/a\rfloor,{\boldsymbol{l}}_{a}{\boldsymbol{0}}^{p}}\right)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\sim}\limits_{a\downarrow 0}}t_{g}\big({\boldsymbol{L}}/\sqrt{A}\big)(A/a)^{\frac{5g-7}{2}+\frac{3b}{4}+p}\,.

Hence, Theorem
5
will be proved if we can show that the
convergence in the last integral expression is dominated. However,
this is a direct consequence of the discussion of the domination
hypothesis around (
72
).
Corollary
6
is proved is a very similar way, this time
summing over all possible values of the perimeters, which results
in the integral with respect to
\mathrm{d}{\boldsymbol{L}}
on
(0,\infty)^{b}
.
